<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:main}
Let $n\geq 3$ and $P(h)$ be a full-dimensional origin-symmetric polytope with the complete irredundant facet representation \eqref{eq:introduction-facet-representation}. Suppose that $\lambda_{1,e}(P(q))\geq \lambda_{1,e}(P(h))$ for every even support vector $q$ sufficiently close to $h$ for which every labeled inequality still defines a facet. Then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \lambda_{1,e}(P(h)) \geq n-1+\frac{1}{n-1}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:introduction-multiplicity-bound}
Under the assumptions of \autoref{thm:main}, if $\lambda_{1,e}(P(h))$ has multiplicity $m$ in the space of even facet functions, then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \lambda_{1,e}(P(h))\geq n-1+\frac{m}{n-1}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Moreover, for any $N\geq n$, every global minimizer $P$ of $\lambda_{1,e}$ in the class of full-dimensional origin-symmetric polytopes in $\mathbb{R}^n$ with at most $N$ pairs of facets, and $m=n-1$, is a parallelotope.

Further details may appear elsewhere.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:sec-2-labeled-facet-network}
For a complete irredundant representation, the facet $F_i$ and its mass $m_i$ are
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  F_i=\{x\in P:\langle x,u_i\rangle=h_i\},\quad
  m_i=h_i\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(F_i).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The vector $h=(h_i)_{i\in J}$ is the \emph{support vector} of this labeled half-space representation.

Two facets are \emph{adjacent} when they meet in a ridge. Adjacent facets $F_i$ and $F_j$ determine
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-2-polytope-conductances}
  R_{ij}=F_i\cap F_j, \quad \theta_{ij}=\arccos\langle u_i,u_j\rangle, \quad c_{ij}=\frac{h_ih_j\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(R_{ij})}{\sin\theta_{ij}}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The number $c_{ij}$ is the \emph{ridge conductance}, with $c_{ij}=0$ for nonadjacent facets. The \emph{labeled facet network} is the graph whose vertices are the facet labels and whose edges join adjacent facets. Its vertex and edge weights are $m_i$ and $c_{ij}$, respectively.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-2-facet-graph-connected}
The facet-adjacency graph of a full-dimensional polytope is connected.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
A translation preserves the facet-adjacency graph. Hence we may assume the origin lies in the interior of $P$. The polar polytope of $P$ is
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  P^{\ast}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^n:\langle x,y\rangle\leq 1\text{ for every }x\in P\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Face-lattice duality sends the facets and ridges of $P$ to the vertices and edges of $P^{\ast}$, respectively; see \cite[p. 109, Eq. (2.28)]{Sch14}. Thus the facet-adjacency graph of $P$ is isomorphic to the vertex-edge graph of $P^{\ast}$, which is connected by Balinski's theorem \cite[p. 95, Thm. 3.14]{Zie95}.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
Suppose that $h$ is even, equivalently $P=-P$. The first nonconstant even eigenvalue of $P$ is
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \lambda_{1,e}(P)
  =\min_{\substack{f\ \mathrm{even}\\f \text{ not constant}}}
  \frac{\mathsf{L}_P(f,f)}
       {\sum_{i\in I}m_i(f_i-\bar{f})^2}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
A \emph{polyhedral fan} in $\mathbb{R}^n$ is a finite collection $\mathcal{F}$ of polyhedral cones such that every face of a cone in $\mathcal{F}$ belongs to $\mathcal{F}$ and the intersection of two cones in $\mathcal{F}$ is a face of each. The fan is \emph{complete} if
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \bigcup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{F}}\zeta=\mathbb{R}^n.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The fan is \emph{simplicial} if every cone in $\mathcal{F}$ is simplicial. A cone in $\mathcal{F}$ is \emph{maximal} if it is maximal under inclusion.

A fan $\mathcal{F}'$ \emph{refines} a fan $\mathcal{F}$ if every cone of $\mathcal{F}'$ is contained in a cone of $\mathcal{F}$. Equivalently, $\mathcal{F}$ \emph{coarsens} $\mathcal{F}'$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
Note that every maximal cone $\zeta$ of a complete fan in $\mathbb{R}^n$ is $n$-dimensional. Indeed, suppose that a maximal cone $\zeta$ had smaller dimension. Let $x\in\operatorname{relint} \zeta$ and $z\notin\operatorname{span}(\zeta)$. For each $k\geq 1$, completeness provides a cone $\mathfrak{C}_k$ of the fan such that $x+k^{-1}z\in\mathfrak{C}_k$. Since the fan is finite, there are a cone $\mathfrak{C}$ and integers $k_r\to\infty$ such that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
x+k_r^{-1}z\in\mathfrak{C}
\quad (r\geq 1).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The cone $\mathfrak{C}$ is closed. Hence $x\in\mathfrak{C}$. Since the face $\zeta\cap\mathfrak{C}$ of $\zeta$ contains $x\in\operatorname{relint} \zeta$, it equals $\zeta$; in particular, $\zeta\subseteq\mathfrak{C}$. On the other hand,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  x+k_r^{-1}z\in\mathfrak{C}\setminus\operatorname{span}(\zeta)
  \quad(r\geq 1).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Therefore, $\zeta\subsetneq\mathfrak{C}$, contrary to the maximality of $\zeta$.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def:sec-3-normal-polytopal-fan}
Let $P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n$ be a nonempty polytope. For $v\in\mathbb{R}^n$, the exposed face of $P$ in direction $v$ is
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  P^v=\{x\in P:\langle x,v\rangle=h_P(v)\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
For a nonempty face $F$ of $P$, including the improper face $F=P$, its \emph{normal cone} is
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  N_P(F)
  =\left\{v\in\mathbb{R}^n:\langle x,v\rangle=h_P(v)
  \text{ for every }x\in F\right\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  N_P(F)=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^n:F\subseteq P^v\}
  =\{v\in\mathbb{R}^n:P^v\cap F=F\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
For a vertex $x$ of $P$, we write $N_P(x)=N_P(\{x\})$. The \emph{normal fan} of $P$, $\mathcal{F}_P$, is the collection of its normal cones \cite[p. 193, Ex. 7.3]{Zie95}. A complete fan is \emph{polytopal} if it is the normal fan of a polytope.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-3-face-normal-cone-duality}
Let $P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n$ be a full-dimensional polytope. The assignment $F\mapsto N_P(F)$ is a bijection from the nonempty faces of $P$ onto the cones of $\mathcal{F}_P$. For any nonempty faces $F$ and $G$:
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  F\subseteq G
  \quad\iff\quad
  N_P(F)\supseteq N_P(G).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Moreover, $\dim F+\dim N_P(F)=n$ for every nonempty face $F$. In addition, for every nonempty face $G$ of $P$ and every $v\in\mathbb{R}^n$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-3-relative-normal-exposed-face}
  v\in\operatorname{relint} N_P(G)
  \quad\iff\quad P^v=G.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:sec-3-normal-cones-form-fan}
The normal cones of a full-dimensional polytope form a complete polyhedral fan.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
By \cite[p. 108, Thm. 2.4.9]{Sch14}, every normal cone is polyhedral. We first show that every face of a normal cone is again a normal cone. Let $F\subseteq G$ be nonempty faces of $P$, and choose $x\in\operatorname{relint} F$ and $y\in\operatorname{relint} G$. For $v\in N_P(F)$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \langle y-x,v\rangle\leq 0,
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
with equality exactly when $G\subseteq P^v$. Thus
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-3-nested-normal-cone-face}
  N_P(G)=N_P(F)\cap\{v\in\mathbb{R}^n:\langle y-x,v\rangle=0\},
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
and $N_P(G)$ is a face of $N_P(F)$.

Conversely, let $\mathfrak{C}$ be a face of $N_P(F)$, choose $v\in\operatorname{relint}\mathfrak{C}$, and put $G=P^v$. Since $v\in N_P(F)$, we have $F\subseteq G$. Moreover, \eqref{eq:sec-3-relative-normal-exposed-face} implies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  v\in\operatorname{relint} N_P(G).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Both $\mathfrak{C}$ and $N_P(G)$ are faces of $N_P(F)$ whose relative interiors contain $v$. Thus $\mathfrak{C}=N_P(G)$.

If $\tilde{F}$ is the smallest face of $P$ containing two nonempty faces $F$ and $G$, then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  v\in N_P(F)\cap N_P(G)
  \quad\iff\quad F\cup G\subseteq P^v
  \quad\iff\quad \tilde{F}\subseteq P^v
  \quad\iff\quad v\in N_P(\tilde{F}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Thus $N_P(F)\cap N_P(G)=N_P(\tilde{F})$ is a face of both cones. Finally,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  v\in N_P(P^v)\quad(v\in\mathbb{R}^n),
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
and the normal cones cover $\mathbb{R}^n$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rem:sec-3-simple-polytopes-simplicial-polytopal-fans}
For a fan $\mathcal{F}$ in $\mathbb{R}^n$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \substack{\mathcal{F}\text{ is complete, simplicial,}\\\text{and polytopal}}
  \quad\iff\quad
  \substack{\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_Q\text{ for some full-dimensional}\\
    \text{simple polytope }Q\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n.}
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{def:sec-3-prescribed-facet-normals}
Consider a finite label set $J$ and pairwise distinct unit vectors $(u_i)_{i\in J}$ that positively span $\mathbb{R}^n$. For $h=(h_i)_{i\in J}\in\mathbb{R}^J$, the associated half-space intersection is defined by
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-3-polytope-support-parameters}
  P(h)=\bigcap_{i\in J}\{x\in\mathbb{R}^n:\langle x,u_i\rangle\leq h_i\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The vector $h$ varies the positions of the supporting hyperplanes while keeping their normals fixed. The half-space intersection $P(h)$ may be empty, and some prescribed inequalities may be redundant.

For $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$, the \emph{slack} of the inequality with label $i$ at $x$ is defined as $h_i-\langle x,u_i\rangle$. If $x\in P(h)$, the inequality is \emph{active at $x$} when its slack vanishes and \emph{strict at $x$} when its slack is positive.
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
For a complete simplicial polytopal fan $\Sigma$ with the prescribed rays, its \emph{simple type chamber} is
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
  =\{h\in\mathbb{R}^J:\text{every inequality defining $P(h)$ is irredundant and }
    \mathcal{F}_{P(h)}=\Sigma\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
It is also called the \emph{type cone} of $\Sigma$. The polytopes represented by $\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$ form what Schneider calls an \emph{$a$-type} \cite[p. 109]{Sch14}.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-3-type-chamber-inequalities}
A vector $h\in\mathbb{R}^J$ belongs to $\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$ if and only if \eqref{eq:sec-3-type-chamber-inequalities} holds. Consequently, $\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$ is a nonempty open convex cone whose closure is a polyhedral cone.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Assume first that $h\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$. By definition, $\mathcal{F}_{P(h)}=\Sigma$, and every prescribed inequality defines a facet of $P(h)$. By \autoref{lem:sec-3-face-normal-cone-duality}, each maximal cone $\zeta$ of $\Sigma$ is the normal cone of a unique vertex of $P(h)$. The facets containing this vertex are precisely those whose outer unit normals have labels in $J_{\zeta}$. Their supporting hyperplanes meet at $x_{\zeta}(h)$. Hence $x_{\zeta}(h)$ is the vertex whose normal cone is $\zeta$. Moreover, for every $j\notin J_{\zeta}$, the inequality $\langle x,u_j\rangle\leq h_j$ in the defining representation of $P(h)$ is strict at $x_{\zeta}(h)$, namely $\langle x_{\zeta}(h),u_j\rangle<h_j$.

Conversely, assume that \eqref{eq:sec-3-type-chamber-inequalities} holds. We first show that $\mathcal{F}_{P(h)}$ and $\Sigma$ have the same maximal cones.  For every maximal cone $\zeta$ of $\Sigma$, \eqref{eq:sec-3-maximal-cone-vertex} implies $\langle x_{\zeta}(h),u_i\rangle=h_i$ for $i\in J_{\zeta}$, whereas \eqref{eq:sec-3-type-chamber-inequalities} states $\langle x_{\zeta}(h),u_j\rangle<h_j$ for $j\notin J_{\zeta}$. It follows that $x_{\zeta}(h)$ belongs to the half-space intersection $P(h)$ defined in \eqref{eq:sec-3-polytope-support-parameters}. Precisely the prescribed inequalities with labels in $J_{\zeta}$ are active at this point. Let $\mathfrak{C}_{\zeta}$ consist of the directions $z$ such that $x_{\zeta}(h)+\varepsilon z\in P(h)$ for all sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$. Since every prescribed inequality with a label outside $J_{\zeta}$ is strict at $x_{\zeta}(h)$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathfrak{C}_{\zeta}
  =\left\{z\in\mathbb{R}^n:\langle z,u_i\rangle\leq 0
  \quad(i\in J_{\zeta})\right\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The image of $\mathfrak{C}_{\zeta}$ under the invertible linear map $U_{\zeta}$ is the negative orthant. Hence $\mathfrak{C}_{\zeta}$ is full dimensional and pointed. The full dimensionality of $\mathfrak{C}_{\zeta}$ implies that $P(h)$ is full dimensional, while its pointedness implies that $x_{\zeta}(h)$ is a vertex.

We next determine the normal cone at this vertex. If $v\in N_{P(h)}(x_{\zeta}(h))$ and $z\in\mathfrak{C}_{\zeta}$, then $x_{\zeta}(h)+\varepsilon z\in P(h)$ for every sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$. By the definition of the normal cone,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  0
  \geq\langle v,x_{\zeta}(h)+\varepsilon z\rangle
    -\langle v,x_{\zeta}(h)\rangle
  =\varepsilon\langle v,z\rangle.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Hence $\langle v,z\rangle\leq 0$. Conversely, suppose that $\langle v,z\rangle\leq 0$ for every $z\in\mathfrak{C}_{\zeta}$. For any $y\in P(h)$, convexity implies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  x_{\zeta}(h)+\varepsilon(y-x_{\zeta}(h))\in P(h)
  \quad(0\leq\varepsilon\leq 1).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Hence $y-x_{\zeta}(h)\in\mathfrak{C}_{\zeta}$ and $\langle v,y-x_{\zeta}(h)\rangle\leq 0$. Since $y$ was arbitrary, $v\in N_{P(h)}(x_{\zeta}(h))$. Therefore,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  N_{P(h)}(x_{\zeta}(h))
  =\{v\in\mathbb{R}^n:\langle v,z\rangle\leq 0
  \quad\text{for every }z\in\mathfrak{C}_{\zeta}\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since the vectors $(u_i)_{i\in J_{\zeta}}$ form a basis of $\mathbb{R}^n$, every $v\in\mathbb{R}^n$ has a unique representation $v=\sum_{i\in J_{\zeta}}a_iu_i$. For $z\in\mathfrak{C}_{\zeta}$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \langle v,z\rangle
  =\sum_{i\in J_{\zeta}}a_i\langle u_i,z\rangle,
  \quad
  \langle u_i,z\rangle\leq 0
  \quad(i\in J_{\zeta}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
If every $a_i$ is nonnegative, then $\langle v,z\rangle\leq 0$ for every $z\in\mathfrak{C}_{\zeta}$. Conversely, suppose that $a_k<0$ for some $k\in J_{\zeta}$. The equality $U_{\zeta}(\mathfrak{C}_{\zeta})=(-\infty,0]^n$ provides a vector $z\in\mathfrak{C}_{\zeta}$ such that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \langle u_k,z\rangle=-1,
  \quad
  \langle u_i,z\rangle=0
  \quad(i\in J_{\zeta}\setminus\{k\}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Then $\langle v,z\rangle=-a_k>0$. Therefore, $\langle v,z\rangle\leq 0$ for every $z\in\mathfrak{C}_{\zeta}$ if and only if all the coefficients $a_i$ are nonnegative. That is,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  N_{P(h)}(x_{\zeta}(h))
  =\operatorname{pos}\{u_i:i\in J_{\zeta}\}
  =\zeta.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Therefore, every maximal cone $\zeta$ of $\Sigma$ is a maximal cone of $\mathcal{F}_{P(h)}$.

For the reverse inclusion, we prove that every vertex of $P(h)$ equals $x_{\zeta}(h)$ for some maximal cone $\zeta$ of $\Sigma$. Let $y$ be a vertex of $P(h)$, and take $v\in\operatorname{relint} N_{P(h)}(y)$. By completeness of $\Sigma$, $v$ belongs to a maximal cone $\zeta$. Since $\zeta=N_{P(h)}(x_{\zeta}(h))$, the point $x_{\zeta}(h)$ belongs to $P(h)^v$. In view of \eqref{eq:sec-3-relative-normal-exposed-face}, $P(h)^v=\{y\}$. Hence $y=x_{\zeta}(h)$.

By \autoref{cor:sec-3-normal-cones-form-fan}, $\mathcal{F}_{P(h)}$ is a polyhedral fan, as is $\Sigma$ by assumption. Every cone in either fan is a face of one of its maximal cones. It follows that the two fans contain the same cones:
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{F}_{P(h)}=\Sigma.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Next, we verify that every prescribed inequality in \eqref{eq:sec-3-polytope-support-parameters} is irredundant; equivalently, each must determine a facet of $P(h)$. Given $i\in J$, completeness of $\Sigma$ provides a maximal cone $\zeta$ containing the ray $\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}u_i$. Since $i\in J_{\zeta}$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \langle x_{\zeta}(h),u_i\rangle=h_i.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Every $x\in P(h)$ satisfies $\langle x,u_i\rangle\leq h_i$, while $x_{\zeta}(h)\in P(h)$ satisfies equality. Hence
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  h_{P(h)}(u_i)=h_i.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since $\mathcal{F}_{P(h)}=\Sigma$ and $u_i\in\operatorname{relint}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}u_i)$, \eqref{eq:sec-3-relative-normal-exposed-face} shows that $P(h)^{u_i}$ is the facet corresponding to this ray. Accordingly, every prescribed inequality determines a facet of $P(h)$, and the equality $\mathcal{F}_{P(h)}=\Sigma$ then implies $h\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$.

We finally describe the chamber $\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$. For every maximal cone $\zeta$ and every $j\notin J_{\zeta}$, consider the linear functional
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \ell_{\zeta,j}(q)
  =q_j-\langle x_{\zeta}(q),u_j\rangle.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:sec-3-maximal-cone-vertex}, $x_{\zeta}(q)$ is independent of $q_j$. Hence $\partial_{q_j}\ell_{\zeta,j}=1$, and $\ell_{\zeta,j}$ is nonzero. The two implications above show that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
  =\left\{q\in\mathbb{R}^J:\ell_{\zeta,j}(q)>0
  \quad(\zeta\text{ a maximal cone of }\Sigma,\ j\notin J_{\zeta})\right\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
These linear functionals form a finite family. Hence $\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$ is an open convex cone. It is nonempty: since $\Sigma$ is polytopal, there is a polytope $Q$ with $\mathcal{F}_Q=\Sigma$. The cone $N_Q(Q)$ is a linear subspace and belongs to the simplicial fan $\Sigma$. Thus $N_Q(Q)=\{0\}$, and $Q$ is full dimensional. The maximal normal cones are simplicial, and face--normal-cone duality implies that $Q$ is simple. Since the rays of $\Sigma$ are generated by the prescribed unit normals,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  Q=P\bigl((h_Q(u_i))_{i\in J}\bigr),
  \quad \bigl(h_Q(u_i)\bigr)_{i\in J}\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Replacing the strict inequalities by non-strict ones, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \overline{\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}}
  =\left\{q\in\mathbb{R}^J:\ell_{\zeta,j}(q)\geq 0
  \quad(\zeta\text{ a maximal cone of }\Sigma,\ j\notin J_{\zeta})\right\},
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
which is a polyhedral cone.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-3-type-chamber-Minkowski-addition}
For $h,q\in\overline{\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}}$ and $s,t\geq 0$ with $s+t>0$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  P(sh+tq)=sP(h)+tP(q).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
By \autoref{lem:sec-3-type-chamber-inequalities}, $sh+tq\in\overline{\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}}$. Let $r\in\overline{\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}}$, and let $\zeta$ be a maximal cone of $\Sigma$. Equation \eqref{eq:sec-3-maximal-cone-vertex} and \autoref{lem:sec-3-type-chamber-inequalities} imply
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \langle x_{\zeta}(r),u_i\rangle&=r_i
  \quad(i\in J_{\zeta}),\\
  \langle x_{\zeta}(r),u_j\rangle&\leq r_j
  \quad(j\notin J_{\zeta}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Hence $x_{\zeta}(r)\in P(r)$. Now note that every $u\in\zeta$ can be written as
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  u=\sum_{i\in J_{\zeta}}\alpha_i u_i,
  \quad
  \alpha_i\geq 0,
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
and every $x\in P(r)$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \langle x,u\rangle
  \leq\sum_{i\in J_{\zeta}}\alpha_i r_i
  =\langle x_{\zeta}(r),u\rangle.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Therefore, $ h_{P(r)}(u)=\langle x_{\zeta}(r),u\rangle$. Moreover, the map $r\mapsto x_{\zeta}(r)$ is linear. Applying the preceding formula to $h$, $q$, and $sh+tq$, we obtain, for every $u\in\zeta$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  h_{P(sh+tq)}(u)
  &=\langle x_{\zeta}(sh+tq),u\rangle\\
  &=s h_{P(h)}(u)+t h_{P(q)}(u)\\
  &=h_{sP(h)+tP(q)}(u).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since the maximal cones of $\Sigma$ cover $\mathbb{R}^n$, the two support functions agree everywhere, and the polytopes are equal.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:sec-3-volume-polynomial}
There is a unique homogeneous polynomial $V_{\Sigma}$ of degree $n$ on $\mathbb{R}^J$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  V_{\Sigma}(h)=\operatorname{Vol}(P(h))
  \quad(h\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Existence follows from the inductive construction in \cite[Thm. 2.1.1]{Tim99}; see also \cite[Sec. 14]{McM93}. Since $\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$ is nonempty and open, two polynomials that agree with volume on $\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$ agree on all of $\mathbb{R}^J$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-3-antipodal-volume-parity}
For the antipodally indexed normals, suppose that $\Sigma$ is invariant under the antipodal map. Then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-3-antipodal-volume-polynomial}
  V_{\Sigma}(\iota z)=V_{\Sigma}(z) \quad \text{for every }z\in\mathbb{R}^I.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
At every even support vector, a mixed derivative in even and odd directions vanishes whenever an odd number of its directions are odd.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The antipodal invariance of $\Sigma$ implies that $\iota\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}=\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$. For $z\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$ and $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  x\in P(\iota z)
  &\iff
  \langle x,u_i\rangle\leq z_{-i}\quad(i\in I)\\
  &\iff
  \langle -x,u_{-i}\rangle\leq z_{-i}\quad(i\in I)\\
  &\iff -x\in P(z).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Thus the defining inequalities imply
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  P(\iota z)=-P(z).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Taking volumes on both sides, we find
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  V_{\Sigma}(\iota z)=\operatorname{Vol}(P(\iota z))=\operatorname{Vol}(-P(z))=V_{\Sigma}(z).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The two polynomials agree on the open set $\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$. Therefore, they agree on $\mathbb{R}^I$.

Let $h\in\mathbb{R}^I$ be even, and suppose that each of $z_1,\ldots,z_k\in\mathbb{R}^I$ is either even or odd. Differentiating \eqref{eq:sec-3-antipodal-volume-polynomial} at $h$ in the directions $z_1,\ldots,z_k$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  D^kV_{\Sigma}(h)[z_1,\ldots,z_k]
  =D^kV_{\Sigma}(h)[\iota z_1,\ldots,\iota z_k].
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
If exactly $r$ directions are odd, multilinearity implies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  D^kV_{\Sigma}(h)[z_1,\ldots,z_k]
  =(-1)^rD^kV_{\Sigma}(h)[z_1,\ldots,z_k].
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Therefore, when $r$ is odd, the derivative vanishes.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:sec-4-logarithmic-volume-Hessian}
For $z\in\mathbb{R}^J$ sufficiently close to the origin, let
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \Xi_h(z)=\log V\bigl(h\odot e^z\bigr), \quad (e^z)_i=e^{z_i}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Then for facet functions $a$ and $b$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
   D^2\Xi_h(0)[a,b]=\frac{n\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}(a,b)-\mathsf{L}(a,b)}{V(h)}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Let $Q(s,t)=h\odot e^{sa+tb}$. At $(0,0)$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \partial_sQ=h\odot a, \quad \partial_tQ=h\odot b, \quad \partial_s\partial_tQ=h\odot(a\odot b).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Moreover, by the chain rule,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\partial_sV(Q(s,t))\right|_{(0,0)}&=DV(h)[h\odot a]=A(a), \\
  \left.\partial_tV(Q(s,t))\right|_{(0,0)}&=DV(h)[h\odot b]=A(b), \\
  \left.\partial_s\partial_tV(Q(s,t))\right|_{(0,0)}&=D^2V(h)[h\odot a,h\odot b]+DV(h)[h\odot(a\odot b)] \\
  &=B(a,b)+A(a\odot b).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Therefore, the Hessian of $\Xi_h$ at the origin is
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  D^2\Xi_h(0)[a,b]=\frac{B(a,b)+A(a\odot b)}{V(h)} - \frac{A(a)A(b)}{V(h)^2}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Now, using $S=nV(h)$ and $\mathsf{L}=(n-1)\mathsf{M}-B$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  B(a,b)+A(a\odot b)&=nA(a\odot b)-\mathsf{L}(a,b), \\
  \frac{B(a,b)+A(a\odot b)}{V(h)}-\frac{A(a)A(b)}{V(h)^2}&=\frac{n}{V(h)}\left(A(a\odot b)-\frac{A(a)A(b)}{S}\right)-\frac{\mathsf{L}(a,b)}{V(h)} \\
  &=\frac{n\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}(a,b)-\mathsf{L}(a,b)}{V(h)}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-4-volume-support-derivatives}
Let $h\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$. Then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \frac{\partial V}{\partial q_i}(h)=\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(F_i).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
For $i\neq j$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \frac{\partial^2V}{\partial q_i\partial q_j}(h)
  =\begin{cases}
 \frac{\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(F_i\cap F_j)}{\sin\theta_{ij}}, & \dim(F_i\cap F_j)=n-2, \\[1.1em]
  0, & \text{otherwise}
  \end{cases}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Here $\theta_{ij}$ is the angle between the outward unit normals $u_i$ and $u_j$.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:sec-4-graph-laplacian}
For all facet functions $a$ and $b$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
   \mathsf{L}(a,b)=\sum_{\{i,j\}\in\binom{J}{2}} c_{ij}(a_i-a_j)(b_i-b_j).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
In particular, $\mathsf{L}$ is positive semidefinite and its kernel consists of the constant facet functions.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
For $i\neq j$, the $(i,j)$ entry of the mass form $\mathsf{M}$ vanishes because $\mathbf{e}_i\odot\mathbf{e}_j=0$, whereas the corresponding entry of $B$ is $c_{ij}$. Thus the relation $\mathsf{L}=(n-1)\mathsf{M}-B$ yields $\mathsf{L}_{ij}=-c_{ij}$. By Euler identities, $\mathsf{L}(\mathbf{1},b)=0$. Hence $\mathsf{L}_{ii}=\sum_{j\neq i}c_{ij}$. Using $c_{ij}=c_{ji}$, the associated bilinear form becomes
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  a^{\mathsf{T}}\mathsf{L}b&=\sum_{i\in J}a_ib_i\sum_{j\neq i}c_{ij}-\sum_{i\neq j}c_{ij}a_ib_j \\
  &=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\neq j}c_{ij}\bigl(a_ib_i+a_jb_j-a_ib_j-a_jb_i\bigr) \\
  &=\sum_{\{i,j\}\in\binom{J}{2}}c_{ij}(a_i-a_j)(b_i-b_j).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathsf{L}(a,a)&=\sum_{\{i,j\}\in\binom{J}{2}}c_{ij}(a_i-a_j)^2\geq 0,\\
  \mathsf{L}(a,a)&=0
  \quad\iff\quad
  a_i=a_j\quad\text{for every edge }\{i,j\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Applying \autoref{lem:sec-2-facet-graph-connected}, we conclude that $\ker\mathsf{L}=\mathbb{R}\mathbf{1}$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:sec-4-logarithmic-volume-Hessian-spectral-gap}
For the antipodally indexed normals, suppose that $\Sigma$ is antipodally invariant and $h\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$ is even. Then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d^2}{dt^2}\right|_{t=0}\log V_{\Sigma}(h\odot e^{ta})\leq 0 \quad\text{for every even facet function }a
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
if and only if $\lambda_{1,e}(P(h))\geq n$.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
By \autoref{prop:sec-4-logarithmic-volume-Hessian}, the first condition is equivalent to
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-4-even-Hessian-form-bound}
  \mathsf{L}(a,a)\geq n\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}(a,a) \quad\text{for every even }a.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Let $a^{\circ}$ be the centered version of $a$ from \eqref{eq:sec-4-centered-representative}. By $\mathsf{L}(\mathbf{1},b)=0$ and \eqref{eq:sec-4-centered-mass-constant-invariance},
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathsf{L}(a,a)=\mathsf{L}(a^{\circ},a^{\circ}),
  \quad
  \widetilde{\mathsf{M}}(a,a)=\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}(a^{\circ},a^{\circ})=A((a^{\circ})^{\odot2}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Thus \eqref{eq:sec-4-even-Hessian-form-bound} is equivalent to
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathsf{L}(b,b)\geq nA(b^{\odot2})
  \quad\text{for every centered even facet function }b.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Now \eqref{eq:introduction-Rayleigh-characterization} becomes
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \lambda_{1,e}(P(h))
  =\min_{\substack{0\neq b\ \text{even}\\A(b)=0}}
  \frac{\mathsf{L}(b,b)}{A(b^{\odot2})}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Therefore, \eqref{eq:sec-4-even-Hessian-form-bound} holds if and only if this minimum is at least $n$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label{def:sec-5-polytopal-tangential-body}
Let $K\subseteq L$ be full-dimensional polytopes in $\mathbb{R}^n$. We say that $L$ is an \emph{$(n-2)$-tangential body of $K$} if
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  K\cap F\neq\varnothing
  \quad\text{for every face $F$ of $L$ satisfying}
  \quad n-2\leq \dim F\leq n-1.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
That is, $K$ meets every facet and every ridge of $L$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-5-common-normal-Minkowski-equality}
Suppose that $h,q\in\mathbb{R}^J$ define full-dimensional polytopes $P=P(h)$ and $Q=P(q)$ and that every prescribed inequality defines a facet of the corresponding polytope. Equality holds in \eqref{eq:sec-5-second-Minkowski} if and only if
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  Q=\gamma P+v,
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
for some $\gamma>0$ and $v\in\mathbb{R}^n$.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Suppose that equality holds. Then \eqref{eq:sec-5-polytopal-equality-condition} holds for some $\gamma>0$ and $v\in\mathbb{R}^n$.

For $i\in J$, the exposed face $\widetilde{F}_i=\widetilde{P}^{u_i}$ is a facet of $\widetilde{P}$ because the prescribed inequality with normal $u_i$ defines a facet of $P$. Applying \eqref{eq:sec-5-polytopal-equality-condition} to $\widetilde{F}_i$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  Q\cap\widetilde{F}_i\neq\varnothing.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
For $x_i\in Q\cap\widetilde{F}_i$, the inclusion $Q\subseteq\widetilde{P}$ implies $h_Q(u_i)\leq h_{\widetilde{P}}(u_i)$, whereas $x_i\in Q\cap\widetilde{F}_i$ implies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  h_Q(u_i)\geq \langle x_i,u_i\rangle=h_{\widetilde{P}}(u_i).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Combining the two inequalities, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  h_Q(u_i)=h_{\widetilde{P}}(u_i) \quad (i\in J).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since both polytopes are intersections of the half-spaces with the prescribed normals $u_i$, these equalities imply $Q=\widetilde{P}=\gamma P+v$.

Conversely, suppose that $Q=\gamma P+v$. Translation invariance and homogeneity of mixed volumes imply
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{V}(P[n-1],Q)=\gamma\operatorname{Vol}(P), \quad
  \mathcal{V}(P[n-2],Q[2])=\gamma^2\operatorname{Vol}(P).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Thus both sides of \eqref{eq:sec-5-second-Minkowski} are equal to $\gamma^2\operatorname{Vol}(P)^2$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:sec-5-support-hessian-kernel}
For $h\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$ with $0\in\operatorname{int}P(h)$, denote the first two logarithmic support forms at $h$ by $A$ and $B$, as in \eqref{eq:sec-4-variation-forms}. Then $D^2V_{\Sigma}(h)$ has exactly one positive eigenvalue, and
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \ker D^2V_{\Sigma}(h)=\mathfrak{t}(\mathbb{R}^n).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Equivalently, every $a\in\mathbb{R}^J$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-5-AF-support-form}
  B(a,a)\leq (n-1)\frac{A(a)^2}{nV_{\Sigma}(h)}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Moreover, equality holds if and only if
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-5-AF-equality}
  a_j=c_0+\frac{\langle v,u_j\rangle}{h_j} \quad \text{for every }j\in J,
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
for some $c_0\in\mathbb{R}$ and $v\in\mathbb{R}^n$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
See the proof of \autoref{thm:sec-8-irredundant-support-Hessian-inequality}: under the invertible diagonal change of variables $a\mapsto h\odot a$, the matrix of $B$ is congruent to $D^2V_{\Sigma}(h)$. Sylvester's law of inertia and the relation $B(a,b)=D^2V_{\Sigma}(h)[h\odot a,h\odot b]$ transfer the positive-eigenvalue count and the kernel description.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:sec-5-strict-even-eigenvalue-bound}
The polytope $P(h)$ satisfies $\lambda_{1,e}(P(h))>n-1$.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
See the proof of \autoref{cor:sec-8-irredundant-strict-even-eigenvalue-bound}.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
\label{def:sec-5-even-facet-functions}
The space of even facet functions and its centered subspace are
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{E}^+
  &=\{a\in\mathbb{R}^I:\iota a=a\},\quad
  \mathcal{E}_0^{+}
  &=\{a\in\mathcal{E}^+:A(a)=0\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-6-derivatives-of-forms}
For facet functions $a$ and $b$ independent of $t$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
   \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}A_t(a)&=A(a\odot g)+B(a,g), \\ \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}B_t(a,b)&=B(a\odot g,b)+B(a,b\odot g)+C(a,b,g), \\ \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}S_t&=nA(g).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Since $h_t=h+t(h\odot g)$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  A_t(a)=DV\bigl(h+t(h\odot g)\bigr)
  [h\odot a+t(h\odot a\odot g)].
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The derivative at zero is
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}A_t(a)
  &=D^2V(h)[h\odot g,h\odot a]+DV(h)[h\odot a\odot g] \\
  &=B(a,g)+A(a\odot g).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Likewise,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  B_t(a,b)=D^2V\bigl(h+t(h\odot g)\bigr)
  [h\odot a+t(h\odot a\odot g),h\odot b+t(h\odot b\odot g)],
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
and its derivative at zero is
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}B_t(a,b)
  &=D^3V(h)[h\odot g,h\odot a,h\odot b] \\
  &\quad+D^2V(h)[h\odot a\odot g,h\odot b] \\
  &\quad+D^2V(h)[h\odot a,h\odot b\odot g] \\
  &=C(a,b,g)+B(a\odot g,b)+B(a,b\odot g).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Finally, $S_t=nV(h_t)$ and
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}S_t=nDV(h)[h\odot g]=nA(g).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-6-rayleigh-stationarity}
For every even facet function $g$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-6-scalar-stationarity}
  C(f,f,g)
  =\beta\bigl\{B(f^{\odot2},g)-A(f^{\odot2}\odot g)\bigr\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Along $h_t=h\odot(\mathbf{1}+tg)$, the centered Rayleigh quotient of $f$ at $h_t$ is
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{R}(t)
  =\frac{\mathsf{L}_t(f,f)}{\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_t(f,f)}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The denominator equals $1$ at $t=0$ and remains positive for sufficiently small $\left|t\right|$. The Rayleigh principle and local minimality imply
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{R}(t)
  &\geq\lambda_{1,e}(P(h_t))
  \geq\lambda
  =\mathcal{R}(0)
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
for all sufficiently small $\left|t\right|$. Hence $\mathcal{R}'(0)=0$.

Note that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_t(f,f)
  &=A_t(f^{\odot2})-\frac{A_t(f)^2}{S_t},\\
  \mathsf{L}_t(f,f)
  &=(n-1)A_t(f^{\odot2})-B_t(f,f).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since $A_0(f)=A(f)=0$, the derivative of $A_t(f)^2/S_t$  at zero vanishes. By \autoref{lem:sec-6-derivatives-of-forms}, the derivatives of $\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_t(f,f)$ and $\mathsf{L}_t(f,f)$  at zero are
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}
  \widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_t(f,f)
  &=A(f^{\odot2}\odot g)+B(f^{\odot2},g),\\
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\mathsf{L}_t(f,f)
  &=(n-1)\bigl\{A(f^{\odot2}\odot g)+B(f^{\odot2},g)\bigr\}\\
  &\quad-B(f\odot g,f)-B(f,f\odot g)-C(f,f,g).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since $f\odot g$ is even, the symmetry of $B$ and \eqref{eq:sec-6-eigen-equations} show that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  B(f\odot g,f)=B(f,f\odot g)
  =\beta\mathsf{M}(f,f\odot g)
  =\beta A(f^{\odot2}\odot g).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Substitution yields
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\mathsf{L}_t(f,f)
  &=(n-1)\bigl\{A(f^{\odot2}\odot g)+B(f^{\odot2},g)\bigr\}\\
  &\quad-2\beta A(f^{\odot2}\odot g)-C(f,f,g).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
At $t=0$, $\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}(f,f)=1$ and $\mathsf{L}(f,f)=\lambda$. Differentiating the Rayleigh quotient, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  0=\mathcal{R}'(0)
  &=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\mathsf{L}_t(f,f)
  -\lambda\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_t(f,f)\\
  &=(n-1-\lambda)\bigl\{A(f^{\odot2}\odot g)+B(f^{\odot2},g)\bigr\}\\
  &\quad-2\beta A(f^{\odot2}\odot g)-C(f,f,g)\\
  &=\beta\bigl\{B(f^{\odot2},g)-A(f^{\odot2}\odot g)\bigr\}-C(f,f,g).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-6-shifted-form-derivative}
Along the support path $h_t=h\odot(\mathbf{1}+tf)$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-6-shifted-form-derivative}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0} \mathcal{D}_{\lambda,t}(f,k)=(1+\beta)\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(f^{\odot2},k)
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
for every even facet function $k$.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Since $f\odot k$ is even, symmetry of $B$, \eqref{eq:sec-6-eigen-equations}, and \eqref{eq:sec-6-eigenfunction-normalization} imply
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  B(f,f)&=\beta,\\
  B(f\odot k,f)&=B(f,f\odot k)
  =\beta\mathsf{M}(f,f\odot k)
  =\beta A(f^{\odot2}\odot k).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Moreover, by \autoref{lem:sec-6-derivatives-of-forms}, \eqref{eq:sec-6-eigen-equations}, and \eqref{eq:sec-6-eigenfunction-normalization},
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}A_t(f)
  &=A(f^{\odot2})+B(f,f)=1+\beta,\\
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}A_t(f\odot k)
  &=A(f^{\odot2}\odot k)+B(f\odot k,f)\\
  &=(1+\beta)A(f^{\odot2}\odot k).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since $A(f)=0$, these formulas determine the derivative of the centered mass form:
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-6-centered-mass-derivative}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_t(f,k)
  &=(1+\beta)\left(A(f^{\odot2}\odot k)-\frac{A(k)}{S}\right)\\
  &=(1+\beta)\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}(f^{\odot2},k).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Next, symmetry of $C$ and the Rayleigh stationarity equation imply
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  C(f,k,f)=C(f,f,k)
  =\beta\bigl\{B(f^{\odot2},k)-A(f^{\odot2}\odot k)\bigr\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Substituting this identity and $B(f,f\odot k)=\beta A(f^{\odot2}\odot k)$ into the variation formula for $B_t$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}B_t(f,k)
  &=B(f^{\odot2},k)+B(f,f\odot k)+C(f,k,f)\\
  &=(1+\beta)B(f^{\odot2},k).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

The energy form of $h_t$ at $(f,k)$ is
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathsf{L}_t(f,k)=(n-1)A_t(f\odot k)-B_t(f,k).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Combining the preceding derivatives yields
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\mathsf{L}_t(f,k)
  =(1+\beta)\mathsf{L}(f^{\odot2},k).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Finally, subtracting $\lambda$ times \eqref{eq:sec-6-centered-mass-derivative} from this identity, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\mathcal{D}_{\lambda,t}(f,k)
  =(1+\beta)\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(f^{\odot2},k).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-6-exact-second-derivative}
The trial Rayleigh quotient satisfies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-6-exact-second-derivative}
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}'(0)=0, \quad \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0)=-\beta(2\beta-1)(\beta+2)\mu_4 +3\beta^2b_2-d_4.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Note that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}(t)-\lambda
  =\frac{\mathcal{D}_{\lambda,t}(u_t,u_t)}
  {\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_t(u_t,u_t)}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:sec-6-D-eigen-equations}, $\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(f,f)=\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(f^{\odot2},f)=\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(f,u_0')=0$. Hence, using \eqref{eq:sec-6-shifted-form-derivative} and symmetry,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\mathcal{D}_{\lambda,t}(u_t,u_t)=0.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Consequently, the first two derivatives at zero are
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}'(0)=0,\quad
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0)
  =\left.\frac{d^2}{dt^2}\right|_{t=0}
  \mathcal{D}_{\lambda,t}(u_t,u_t).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

In the remainder of the proof, dots indicate differentiation of the form only, not of its arguments. Thus, for facet functions $a$ and $b$ independent of $t$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \dot{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda,0}(a,b)
  =\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\mathcal{D}_{\lambda,t}(a,b),\quad
  \ddot{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda,0}(a,b)
  =\left.\frac{d^2}{dt^2}\right|_{t=0}\mathcal{D}_{\lambda,t}(a,b).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Applying \eqref{eq:sec-6-shifted-form-derivative} with $k=u_0'$ and using $u_0'=-(1+\beta)f^{\odot2}$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \dot{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda,0}(f,u_0')
  &=-(1+\beta)^2\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(f^{\odot2},f^{\odot2}),\\
  \mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(u_0',u_0')
  &=(1+\beta)^2\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(f^{\odot2},f^{\odot2}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
We calculate
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-6-trial-second-reduction}
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0)
  &=\ddot{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda,0}(f,f)
  +4\dot{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda,0}(f,u_0')
  +2\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(u_0',u_0')\\
  &=\ddot{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda,0}(f,f)
  -2(1+\beta)^2\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(f^{\odot2},f^{\odot2}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Next we evaluate $\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(f^{\odot2},f^{\odot2})$ and $\ddot{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda,0}(f,f)$.

First, using the definitions of $\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}$, $\mathsf{L}$, and $\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}$, together with $A(f^{\odot2})=1$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(f^{\odot2},f^{\odot2})
  &=\mathsf{L}(f^{\odot2},f^{\odot2})
  -\lambda\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}(f^{\odot2},f^{\odot2})\\
  &=(n-1)\mathsf{M}(f^{\odot2},f^{\odot2})-B(f^{\odot2},f^{\odot2})\\
  &\quad-\lambda\left(\mathsf{M}(f^{\odot2},f^{\odot2})
  -\frac{A(f^{\odot2})^2}{S}\right)\\
  &=\beta\mu_4-b_2+\frac{\lambda}{S}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Next, to calculate $\ddot{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda,0}(f,f)$, we rewrite $\mathcal{D}_{\lambda,t}(f,f)$ as
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{D}_{\lambda,t}(f,f)
  &=\mathsf{L}_t(f,f)-\lambda\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_t(f,f)\\
  &=(n-1)A_t(f^{\odot2})-B_t(f,f)
  -\lambda\left(A_t(f^{\odot2})-\frac{A_t(f)^2}{S_t}\right)\\
  &=\beta A_t(f^{\odot2})-B_t(f,f)
  +\lambda\frac{A_t(f)^2}{S_t}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Recall from the proof of \autoref{lem:sec-6-shifted-form-derivative} that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \dot{A}_0(f)=A(f^{\odot2})+B(f,f)=1+\beta.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since $A_0(f)=0$, we have
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d^2}{dt^2}\right|_{t=0}
  \frac{A_t(f)^2}{S_t}
  =\frac{2\dot{A}_0(f)^2}{S}
  =\frac{2(1+\beta)^2}{S}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

The formula for $\ddot{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda,0}(f,f)$ now requires the second derivatives of $A_t(f^{\odot2})$ and $B_t(f,f)$. The eigenvalue equation and the Rayleigh stationarity equation with $g=f^{\odot2}$ imply
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  B(f^{\odot3},f)=\beta\mu_4,\quad
  C(f^{\odot2},f,f)=\beta(b_2-\mu_4).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Along $h_t=h+t(h\odot f)$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  A_t(f^{\odot2})
  &=DV\bigl(h+t(h\odot f)\bigr)
  [h\odot f^{\odot2}+t(h\odot f^{\odot3})],\\
  B_t(f,f)
  &=D^2V\bigl(h+t(h\odot f)\bigr)
  [h\odot f+t(h\odot f^{\odot2}),h\odot f+t(h\odot f^{\odot2})].
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Differentiating twice, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \ddot{A}_0(f^{\odot2})
  &=2B(f^{\odot3},f)+C(f^{\odot2},f,f)\\
  &=\beta(b_2+\mu_4),\\
  \ddot{B}_0(f,f)
  &=2B(f^{\odot2},f^{\odot2})+4C(f^{\odot2},f,f)+D(f,f,f,f)\\
  &=(2+4\beta)b_2-4\beta\mu_4+d_4.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Substituting into the formula for $\mathcal{D}_{\lambda,t}(f,f)$, we find
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \ddot{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda,0}(f,f)
  &=\beta\ddot{A}_0(f^{\odot2})-\ddot{B}_0(f,f)
  +\frac{2\lambda(1+\beta)^2}{S}\\
  &=\beta^2(b_2+\mu_4)
  -\bigl\{(2+4\beta)b_2-4\beta\mu_4+d_4\bigr\}
  +\frac{2\lambda(1+\beta)^2}{S}\\
  &=(\beta^2-4\beta-2)b_2
  +\beta(\beta+4)\mu_4-d_4
  +\frac{2\lambda(1+\beta)^2}{S}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Finally, substituting the formulas for $\ddot{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda,0}(f,f)$ and $\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(f^{\odot2},f^{\odot2})$ into \eqref{eq:sec-6-trial-second-reduction}, we arrive at
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0)
  &=\bigl\{\beta^2-4\beta-2+2(1+\beta)^2\bigr\}b_2\\
  &\quad+\bigl\{\beta(\beta+4)-2\beta(1+\beta)^2\bigr\}\mu_4-d_4\\
  &=3\beta^2b_2-\beta(2\beta-1)(\beta+2)\mu_4-d_4.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-7-polytope-algebra-duality}
For every $0\leq k\leq n$, multiplication followed by evaluation on $V$ defines a nondegenerate pairing
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-7-polytope-algebra-duality}
  \mathcal{A}^k(V)\times\mathcal{A}^{n-k}(V)\to\mathbb{R}, \quad
  (\alpha,\gamma)\mapsto(\alpha\gamma)V.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Here nondegenerate means that every nonzero $\alpha\in\mathcal{A}^k(V)$ pairs nontrivially with some $\gamma\in\mathcal{A}^{n-k}(V)$, and conversely with the two factors interchanged.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Since $\operatorname{Ann}(V)$ is an ideal, replacing $\Theta$ or $\Psi$ by another representative changes $\Theta\Psi$ by an operator in $\operatorname{Ann}(V)$, which vanishes on $V$.

For $0\neq\alpha=[\Theta]\in\mathcal{A}^k(V)$ represented by a homogeneous operator $\Theta$ of degree $k$, the polynomial $\Theta(V)$ is nonzero and homogeneous of degree $n-k$. For a multi-index $\nu=(\nu_i)_{i\in I}$ of nonnegative integers, put
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left|\nu\right|=\sum_{i\in I}\nu_i,\quad
  \nu!=\prod_{i\in I}\nu_i!,\quad
  q^{\nu}=\prod_{i\in I}q_i^{\nu_i},\quad
  \partial_q^{\nu}=\prod_{i\in I}\partial_{q_i}^{\nu_i}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The homogeneous expansion of $\Theta(V)$ is
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \Theta(V)(q)=\sum_{\left|\nu\right|=n-k}c_{\nu} q^{\nu}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since $\Theta(V)$ is nonzero, some multi-index $\nu^{*}$ satisfies $\left|\nu^{*}\right|=n-k$ and $c_{\nu^{*}}\neq 0$. Whenever $\partial_q^{\nu^{*}}q^{\nu}\neq 0$, we have $\nu_i\geq \nu_i^{*}$ for every $i\in I$. Both multi-indices have total degree $n-k$. Therefore, the coordinatewise inequalities force $\nu=\nu^{*}$. Consequently,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \partial_q^{\nu^{*}}\Theta(V)
  =(\nu^{*})!c_{\nu^{*}}\neq 0.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Therefore, the class $[\partial_q^{\nu^{*}}]\in\mathcal{A}^{n-k}(V)$ pairs nontrivially with $\alpha$. Applying the same argument to a nonzero class in $\mathcal{A}^{n-k}(V)$ proves nondegeneracy in the other factor.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:sec-7-degree-two-HRM}
Suppose that $n\geq 4$. If $\Gamma\in\mathcal{A}^2(V)$ satisfies $\Gamma\omega^{n-3}=0$, then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \bigl(\Gamma^2\omega^{n-4}\bigr)V\geq 0.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Equality holds if and only if $\Gamma=0$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{rem:sec-7-mixed-volume-HR}
Assume that $n\geq 4$. The degree-two Hodge--Riemann inequality of McMullen and Timorin admits the following mixed-volume formulation; see van Handel \cite[Thm. 3.1]{vH23a}. In the specialization relevant here, let $K_1,\ldots,K_m$ have the same normal fan $\Sigma$ as the fixed simple polytope $P$, and let $x_1,\ldots,x_m\in\mathbb{R}$. If
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \sum_{i=1}^m x_i\mathcal{V}(K_i[2],M,P[n-3])=0
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
for every polytope $M$ with normal fan $\Sigma$, then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \sum_{i,j=1}^m x_ix_j\mathcal{V}(K_i[2],K_j[2],P[n-4])\geq 0.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Write $q^{(i)}=(h_{K_i}(u_j))_{j\in I}$ for the support vector of $K_i$, and set
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \Gamma=\sum_{i=1}^m x_i[\partial_{q^{(i)}}]^2.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The mixed-volume derivative identity and \autoref{lem:sec-7-polytope-algebra-duality} imply that the hypothesis is equivalent to $\Gamma\omega^{n-3}=0$, while the sum in the conclusion equals $(\Gamma^2\omega^{n-4})V/n!$. Every degree-two class admits such a representation by polarization \cite[Lem. 3.3]{vH23a}. Equality holds if and only if $\Gamma=0$; see \cite[Thm. 3.2]{vH23a}.

This formulation assumes a common simple normal fan. At nonsimple support vectors, we apply the inequality inside an incident simple type chamber and pass the fourth-derivative bound to the boundary; see \autoref{lem:sec-10-incident-fourth-derivative-lower-bound}.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-7-repeated-radial-derivatives}
Let $0\leq k\leq n$ and $z_1,\ldots,z_k\in\mathbb{R}^I$. Then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-7-repeated-radial-derivatives}
  \bigl(
    \partial_{z_1}\cdots\partial_{z_k}
    (\partial_h)^{n-k}
  \bigr)V
  =(n-k)!D^kV(h)[z_1,\ldots,z_k].
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Set $ p=\partial_{z_1}\cdots\partial_{z_k}V$. This is a homogeneous polynomial of degree $n-k$. Since the differential operators have constant coefficients and commute,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \bigl(
    \partial_{z_1}\cdots\partial_{z_k}
    (\partial_h)^{n-k}
  \bigr)V
  =(\partial_h)^{n-k}p.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The right-hand side is constant and may be evaluated at the origin:
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  (\partial_h)^{n-k}p
  =\left.\frac{d^{n-k}}{dt^{n-k}}\right|_{t=0}p(th)
  =(n-k)!p(h)
  =(n-k)!D^kV(h)[z_1,\ldots,z_k].
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-7-fourth-derivative-lower-bound}
For the even facet function $\eta$ defined by \eqref{eq:sec-7-eta-definition}, the fourth derivative satisfies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-7-fourth-derivative-lower-bound}
  d_4\geq (n-2)(n-3)B(\eta,\eta).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
For $n\geq 4$, equality holds if and only if $\Gamma=0$.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
If $n=3$, the volume polynomial has degree three. Hence both sides of \eqref{eq:sec-7-fourth-derivative-lower-bound} vanish. 
Suppose that $n\geq 4$. By construction, the class $\Gamma$ in \eqref{eq:sec-7-Gamma-definition} is primitive. The first inequality below follows from \autoref{thm:sec-7-degree-two-HRM}. We then expand $\Gamma$, apply \autoref{lem:sec-7-repeated-radial-derivatives} term by term, and use \eqref{eq:sec-7-eta-definition} with $g=\eta$:
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  0
  &\leq \frac{1}{(n-4)!}
    \bigl(\Gamma^2\omega^{n-4}\bigr)V\\
  &=\frac{1}{(n-4)!}\bigl(
    \mathfrak{d}(f)^4\omega^{n-4}
    -2\mathfrak{d}(f)^2\mathfrak{d}(\eta)\omega^{n-3}
    +\mathfrak{d}(\eta)^2\omega^{n-2}\bigr)V\\
  &=d_4-2(n-3)C(f,f,\eta)
    +(n-3)(n-2)B(\eta,\eta)\\
  &=d_4-2(n-3)(n-2)B(\eta,\eta)
    +(n-3)(n-2)B(\eta,\eta)\\
  &=d_4-(n-3)(n-2)B(\eta,\eta).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-7-trial-Rayleigh-second-variation-bound}
For the trial vector $u_t$ defined in \eqref{eq:sec-6-trial-vector}, the trial quotient $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-7-final-second-variation}
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}'(0)=0,
  \quad
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0)
 \leq
  \frac{2\beta\lambda}{S(n-1)}
  \bigl(1+(n-1)\beta\bigr).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Equality in the estimate for $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0)$ holds if and only if $f^{\odot2}=\frac{1}{S}\mathbf{1}$ and, when $n\geq 4$, the primitive class $\Gamma$ in \eqref{eq:sec-7-Gamma-definition} vanishes.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
$\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}'(0)=0$ follows from \eqref{eq:sec-6-exact-second-derivative}. Recall from \autoref{sec:support-hessian} that $(e_k)$ is an $\mathsf{M}$-orthonormal basis of $\mathcal{E}_0^{+}$ satisfying $B(e_j,e_k)=\rho_k\delta_{jk}$, where $\rho_k=n-1-\lambda_k$. The spectral order \eqref{eq:sec-5-B-spectral-order} implies $\rho_k\leq \beta<0$. 

The vector $w=f^{\odot2}$ is even and $A(w)=1$. Thus $w-S^{-1}\mathbf{1}\in\mathcal{E}_0^{+}$, and
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  w=\frac{1}{S}\mathbf{1}+\sum_k\gamma_ke_k,
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
for some constants $\gamma_k$. Also note that \eqref{eq:sec-6-scalar-stationarity} and \eqref{eq:sec-7-eta-definition} imply
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-7-eta-spectral-identity}
  (n-2)B(\eta,g)
  =\beta\bigl(B(w,g)-\mathsf{M}(w,g)\bigr)
  \quad\text{for every even }g.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

We next determine the coefficients in the expansion of $\eta$:
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \eta=c_0\mathbf{1}+\sum_k\xi_ke_k.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Taking $g=\mathbf{1}$ in \eqref{eq:sec-7-eta-spectral-identity} and noting that $A(\mathbf{1})=S$ and $A(e_k)=B(\mathbf{1},e_k)=0$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  (n-2)(n-1)c_0S
  &=\beta\bigl(B(w,\mathbf{1})-\mathsf{M}(w,\mathbf{1})\bigr)\\
  &=\beta\bigl((n-1)A(w)-A(w)\bigr)
  =\beta(n-2).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
It follows that $c_0=\frac{\beta}{(n-1)S}$.

Taking $g=e_j$ in \eqref{eq:sec-7-eta-spectral-identity} yields
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  (n-2)\rho_j\xi_j
  =(n-2)B(\eta,e_j)
  &=\beta\bigl(B(w,e_j)-\mathsf{M}(w,e_j)\bigr)\\
  &=\beta(\rho_j-1)\gamma_j.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since $\rho_j\neq 0$, we have $\xi_j=\frac{\beta}{n-2}\frac{\rho_j-1}{\rho_j}\gamma_j$. Hence
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \eta=\frac{\beta}{(n-1)S}\mathbf{1}+\frac{\beta}{n-2}\sum_k\frac{\rho_k-1}{\rho_k}\gamma_ke_k.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Now, the expansions of $w$ and $\eta$ yield
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mu_4=\mathsf{M}(w,w)
  &=\frac{1}{S}+\sum_k\gamma_k^2,\\
  b_2=B(w,w)
  &=\frac{n-1}{S}+\sum_k\rho_k\gamma_k^2,\\
  B(\eta,\eta)
  &=\frac{\beta^2}{(n-1)S}
    +\frac{\beta^2}{(n-2)^2}
    \sum_k\frac{(\rho_k-1)^2}{\rho_k}\gamma_k^2.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Recall from \autoref{lem:sec-6-exact-second-derivative} that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0)
  =-\beta(2\beta-1)(\beta+2)\mu_4
  +3\beta^2b_2-d_4.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Substituting the formulas for $\mu_4$, $b_2$, and $B(\eta,\eta)$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-7-second-variation-remainder}
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0)
  &-\frac{2\beta\lambda}{(n-1)S}
  \bigl(1+(n-1)\beta\bigr)\\
  &=-\bigl(d_4-(n-2)(n-3)B(\eta,\eta)\bigr)
  -\frac{\beta^2(2n-3)}{n-2}
    \sum_k(\beta-\rho_k)\gamma_k^2\\
  &\quad-\frac{\beta(\beta-1)\lambda}{n-2}
    \sum_k\gamma_k^2-\frac{\beta^2(n-3)}{n-2}
    \sum_k(\rho_k^{-1}-\beta^{-1})\gamma_k^2.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
By \autoref{lem:sec-7-fourth-derivative-lower-bound}, the first term on the right is nonpositive. Moreover, the remaining three terms are nonpositive, and the estimate follows.

It remains to determine when equality holds. Equality forces $\gamma_k=0$ for every $k$. This is equivalent to $f^{\odot2}=S^{-1}\mathbf{1}$. If $n=3$, the first term on the right-hand side vanishes because $d_4=0$. If $n\geq 4$, \autoref{lem:sec-7-fourth-derivative-lower-bound} shows that $d_4=(n-2)(n-3)B(\eta,\eta)$ if and only if $\Gamma=0$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:sec-7-fixed-fan-lower-bound}
Suppose that $n\geq 3$ and that an even $h\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \lambda_{1,e}(P(q))\geq \lambda_{1,e}(P(h))
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
for every even $q\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$ sufficiently close to $h$. Then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-7-fixed-fan-lower-bound}
  \lambda_{1,e}(P(h))\geq n-1+\frac{1}{n-1}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Let $f$ be a first nonconstant even eigenfunction normalized by \eqref{eq:sec-6-eigenfunction-normalization}, and consider the support path $h_t=h\odot(\mathbf{1}+tf)$ with the trial quotient $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}$ from \autoref{lem:sec-7-trial-Rayleigh-second-variation-bound}. By \autoref{cor:sec-5-strict-even-eigenvalue-bound}, $\lambda=\lambda_{1,e}(P(h))>n-1$. If \eqref{eq:sec-7-fixed-fan-lower-bound} failed, then $-1/(n-1)<\beta<0$, and \eqref{eq:sec-7-final-second-variation} would imply $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0)<0$. For all sufficiently small $\left|t\right|$, the vector $h_t$ is even and belongs to $\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$. The Rayleigh principle and local minimality yield $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}(t)\geq \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}(0)$. Thus $t=0$ is a local minimum of $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}$, contrary to $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0)<0$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
\label{def:sec-8-irredundant-support-domain}
The \emph{irredundant support domain} is
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}
  =\left\{h\in\mathbb{R}^J:
    \begin{array}{l}
    0\in\operatorname{int}P(h),\text{ and}\\
    \text{every labeled inequality in
      \eqref{eq:sec-3-polytope-support-parameters} defines a facet of }P(h)
    \end{array}
  \right\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-8-irredundant-open}
The domain $\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$ is an open convex cone.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Let $h\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$. For $i\in J$, write
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  H_i(q)&=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^n:\langle x,u_i\rangle=q_i\},\quad
  F_i(q)&=P(q)\cap H_i(q).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Choose $y^{(i)}(h)\in\operatorname{relint} F_i(h)$. For $q$ near $h$, set $y^{(i)}(q)=y^{(i)}(h)+(q_i-h_i)u_i$. Since $u_i$ is a unit vector,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \langle y^{(i)}(q),u_i\rangle&=q_i,\\
  q_j-\langle y^{(i)}(q),u_j\rangle
  &=h_j-\langle y^{(i)}(h),u_j\rangle
    +(q_j-h_j)-(q_i-h_i)\langle u_i,u_j\rangle
  \quad(j\neq i).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The first term on the right is positive because $y^{(i)}(h)\in\operatorname{relint} F_i(h)$. Finiteness of $J$ provides a neighborhood of $h$ in which all these slacks remain positive. Thus $F_i(q)$ contains a relatively open neighborhood of $y^{(i)}(q)$ in $H_i(q)$ and is a facet. After shrinking the neighborhood, every coordinate of $q$ is positive. Hence $0\in\operatorname{int}P(q)$. It follows that $\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$ is open.

Positive dilations preserve $\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$. To prove closure under addition, consider $h,h'\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$. For each $i\in J$, take
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  y\in\operatorname{relint} F_i(h),\quad
  y'\in\operatorname{relint} F_i(h').
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
At $y+y'$, the $i$th inequality defining $P(h+h')$ is an equality, while all the other defining inequalities are strict. Therefore, this inequality defines a facet of $P(h+h')$. Every coordinate of $h+h'$ is positive. Hence $0\in\operatorname{int}P(h+h')$. Thus $h+h'\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$, and closure under addition and positive dilation makes $\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$ a convex cone.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-8-vertex-simplicity-criterion}
Let $q\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$. For every $\tilde{J}\in\mathcal{B}$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-basis-intersection-in-polytope}
  x_{\tilde{J}}(q)\in P(q)
  \quad\iff\quad
  \delta_{\tilde{J},k}(q)\geq 0
  \quad(k\notin\tilde{J}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Moreover, $P(q)$ is simple if and only if, for every $\tilde{J}\in\mathcal{B}$, either
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-nonvertex-alternative}
  \delta_{\tilde{J},k}(q)<0
  \quad\text{for some }k\notin\tilde{J},
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
or
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-simple-vertex-alternative}
  \delta_{\tilde{J},k}(q)>0
  \quad(k\notin\tilde{J}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
In the latter case, $x_{\tilde{J}}(q)$ is a vertex whose incident facets are precisely those indexed by $\tilde{J}$.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
By definition, $\langle x_{\tilde{J}}(q),u_i\rangle=q_i$ for $i\in\tilde{J}$. For $k\notin\tilde{J}$, the inequality $\langle x_{\tilde{J}}(q),u_k\rangle\leq q_k$ is equivalent to $\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(q)\geq 0$. Hence $x_{\tilde{J}}(q)$ belongs to $P(q)$ exactly under the condition in \eqref{eq:sec-8-basis-intersection-in-polytope}.

When $x_{\tilde{J}}(q)\in P(q)$, $x_{\tilde{J}}(q)$ is a vertex: the $n$ supporting hyperplanes with labels in $\tilde{J}$ have independent normals and meet only at this point. Since $q\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$, the facets containing this vertex have label set
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \tilde{J}\cup\{k\notin\tilde{J}:\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(q)=0\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Now we prove the second part of the lemma. Assume that $P(q)$ is simple. If $x_{\tilde{J}}(q)\notin P(q)$, then \eqref{eq:sec-8-basis-intersection-in-polytope} implies that $\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(q)<0$ for some $k\notin\tilde{J}$. If $x_{\tilde{J}}(q)\in P(q)$, all the remaining slacks are nonnegative. Simplicity and the preceding description of the incident facets imply $\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(q)>0$ for all $k\notin\tilde{J}$. Hence \eqref{eq:sec-8-simple-vertex-alternative} holds.

Conversely, suppose that the two alternatives hold for every $\tilde{J}\in\mathcal{B}$. Given a vertex $y$ of $P(q)$, the outer normals of the facets containing $y$ span $\mathbb{R}^n$. Select $n$ such facets whose outer normals form a basis, and let $\tilde{J}$ be their label set. Since $y$ belongs to these facets,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \langle y,u_i\rangle=q_i
  \quad(i\in\tilde{J}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
These equations have the unique solution $x_{\tilde{J}}(q)$. Hence $y=x_{\tilde{J}}(q)$. Since $y\in P(q)$, \eqref{eq:sec-8-nonvertex-alternative} is impossible. Therefore, $\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(q)>0$ for every $k\notin\tilde{J}$, and no facet with a label outside $\tilde{J}$ contains $y$. Thus exactly the $n$ facets indexed by $\tilde{J}$ contain $y$, and $P(q)$ is simple.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-8-simple-chamber-approximation}
Let $h\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$.
For every $z\in\mathbb{R}^J\setminus\mathcal{N}_h$, there are $t_0>0$ and a simple type chamber $\sigma=\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$ such that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  h+tz\in\sigma\cap\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}
  \quad(0<t<t_0).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Consequently, $P(h+tz)$ is simple for $0<t<t_0$ and $h\in\overline{\sigma}$.
The same conclusion holds for $h\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}^I$, with the notation just introduced on $\mathbb{R}^I$.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Let $z\in\mathbb{R}^J\setminus\mathcal{N}_h$. For every $\tilde{J}\in\mathcal{B}$ and $k\notin\tilde{J}$, linearity of the slack functional implies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \delta_{\tilde{J},k}(h+tz)
  =\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(h)+t\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(z).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
If $\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(h)=0$, the definition of $\mathcal{N}_h$ ensures that $\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(z)\neq 0$. Hence $\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(h+tz)$ has constant sign for every $t>0$. If $\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(h)\neq 0$, the sign of $\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(h+tz)$ agrees with that of $\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(h)$ for all sufficiently small $t>0$. The family of slack functionals is finite, and $\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$ is open. Therefore, there is $t_0>0$ such that $h+tz\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$ and every $\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(h+tz)$ is nonzero with a sign independent of $t$ whenever $0<t<t_0$. Now \autoref{lem:sec-8-vertex-simplicity-criterion} shows that $P(h+tz)$ is simple for $0<t<t_0$. The same lemma shows that $\tilde{J}$ labels the facets incident to a vertex precisely when
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \delta_{\tilde{J},k}(h+tz)>0
  \quad(k\notin\tilde{J}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
In particular, the vertex--facet incidences are also independent of $t$. For every such $\tilde{J}$, the corresponding normal cone is $\operatorname{pos}\{u_i:i\in\tilde{J}\}$. Thus the maximal cones of the normal fan are independent of $t$. Since a complete fan is determined by its maximal cones, all these polytopes have the same normal fan, denoted by $\Sigma$. Then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-generic-ray-chamber}
  h+tz\in\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
  \quad(0<t<t_0)
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
and we have $h\in\overline{\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}}$. The proof on $\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}^I$ is identical.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:sec-8-support-derivatives}
For $h\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$ and $i\neq j$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \frac{\partial V}{\partial q_i}(h)
  &=\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(F_i),\\
  \frac{\partial^2V}{\partial q_i\partial q_j}(h)
  &=\begin{cases}
    \dfrac{\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(F_i\cap F_j)}{\sin\theta_{ij}},
      & \dim(F_i\cap F_j)=n-2,\\[1.1em]
    0, & \text{otherwise}.
  \end{cases}
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Here $F_i$ and $F_j$ are the corresponding facets of $P(h)$.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
\autoref{thm:sec-9-C2-regularity} establishes that $V\in C^2(\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}})$. Let $h\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$. By \autoref{lem:sec-8-simple-chamber-approximation}, some simple type chamber $\sigma=\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}$ is incident to $h$ and contains a sequence
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  h^{(\nu)}\in\sigma\cap\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}},
  \quad
  h^{(\nu)}\to h.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Denote the chamber volume polynomial of $\sigma$ by $\widehat{V}_{\sigma}=V_{\Sigma}$. Since $V=\widehat{V}_{\sigma}$ on $\sigma\cap\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  D^rV(h^{(\nu)})=D^r\widehat{V}_{\sigma}(h^{(\nu)})
  \quad(0\leq r\leq 2).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The $C^2$ regularity of $V$ and the continuity of the derivatives of $\widehat{V}_{\sigma}$ at $h$ imply
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-incident-derivative-agreement}
  D^rV(h)
  =\lim_{\nu\to\infty}D^rV(h^{(\nu)})
  =\lim_{\nu\to\infty}D^r\widehat{V}_{\sigma}(h^{(\nu)})
  =D^r\widehat{V}_{\sigma}(h)
  \quad(0\leq r\leq 2).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

For each $\nu$, denote the corresponding facets and their intersections by
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  F_i^{(\nu)}&=P(h^{(\nu)})\cap\{x\in\mathbb{R}^n:\langle x,u_i\rangle=h_i^{(\nu)}\},\\
  F_i&=P(h)\cap\{x\in\mathbb{R}^n:\langle x,u_i\rangle=h_i\},\\
  R_{ij}^{(\nu)}&=F_i^{(\nu)}\cap F_j^{(\nu)}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The convergence $h^{(\nu)}\to h$ implies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-ridge-volume-convergence}
  F_i^{(\nu)}&\to F_i
    \quad\text{in the Hausdorff metric},\\
  \mathcal{H}^{n-2}(R_{ij}^{(\nu)})
  &\to
    \begin{cases}
      \mathcal{H}^{n-2}(F_i\cap F_j),
        &\dim(F_i\cap F_j)=n-2,\\
      0, &\text{otherwise},
    \end{cases}
    \quad i\neq j.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

For every $\nu$, \autoref{lem:sec-4-volume-support-derivatives} applies to $\widehat{V}_{\sigma}$ at $h^{(\nu)}$. Taking $\nu\to\infty$ in its formulas and using \eqref{eq:sec-8-incident-derivative-agreement}--\eqref{eq:sec-8-ridge-volume-convergence} yields the two identities in the proposition.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:sec-8-irredundant-support-Hessian-inequality}
For $h\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$, every facet function $a$ satisfies the \emph{support-Hessian inequality}
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-support-Hessian-inequality}
   B(a,a)\leq (n-1)\frac{A(a)^2}{S}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Equality holds if and only if
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-irredundant-AF-equality}
  a_i=c_0+\frac{\langle v,u_i\rangle}{h_i}
  \quad(i\in J),
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
for some $c_0\in\mathbb{R}$ and $v\in\mathbb{R}^n$. Consequently, the matrix of $B$ in the logarithmic support coordinates has exactly one positive eigenvalue, and
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \ker B=\{a\in\mathbb{R}^J:h\odot a\in\mathfrak{t}(\mathbb{R}^n)\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
For $q_0,q_1\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$ and $0\leq t\leq 1$, we have
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  (1-t)P(q_0)+tP(q_1)
  \subseteq P((1-t)q_0+tq_1).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
From the Brunn--Minkowski inequality and monotonicity of volume, it follows that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  V((1-t)q_0+tq_1)^{\frac{1}{n}}
  \geq (1-t)V(q_0)^{\frac{1}{n}}+tV(q_1)^{\frac{1}{n}}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
That is, $V^{\frac{1}{n}}$ is concave on the open convex cone $\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$. For $z=h\odot a$, the definitions of $A$, $B$, and $S$ yield
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  0\geq D^2(V^{\frac{1}{n}})(h)[z,z]
  =\frac{V(h)^{\frac{1-n}{n}}}{n}
    \left(
      B(a,a)-(n-1)\frac{A(a)^2}{S}
    \right).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

We next characterize equality. Suppose that equality holds in \eqref{eq:sec-8-support-Hessian-inequality}, and set $z=h\odot a$. Choose $\mathcal{U}$ as in \eqref{eq:sec-9-local-incident-chambers} and $\varepsilon>0$ small enough that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  q=h+\varepsilon z\in\mathcal{U},
  \quad
  P=P(h),
  \quad
  Q=P(q).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Then $h$ and $q$ lie in the closure of a common simple type chamber $\sigma$. By \autoref{lem:sec-3-type-chamber-Minkowski-addition},
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-irredundant-Minkowski-segment}
  P((1-t)h+tq)=(1-t)P+tQ
  \quad(0\leq t\leq 1).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Since $(1-t)h+tq=h+t\varepsilon z$, taking volumes in \eqref{eq:sec-8-irredundant-Minkowski-segment} yields
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  V(h+t\varepsilon z)=\operatorname{Vol}((1-t)P+tQ)
  \quad(0\leq t\leq 1).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The mixed-volume expansion of the right-hand side is
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \operatorname{Vol}((1-t)P+tQ)
  =\sum_{k=0}^n\binom{n}{k}(1-t)^{n-k}t^k
    \mathcal{V}(P[n-k],Q[k]).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
At $t=0$, only the terms with $k\leq 2$ contribute to its first two derivatives. With
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  V_0=\operatorname{Vol}(P),\quad
  V_1=\mathcal{V}(P[n-1],Q),\quad
  V_2=\mathcal{V}(P[n-2],Q[2]),
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
these derivatives are
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\operatorname{Vol}((1-t)P+tQ)
  &=n(V_1-V_0),\\
  \left.\frac{d^2}{dt^2}\right|_{t=0}\operatorname{Vol}((1-t)P+tQ)
  &=n(n-1)(V_2-2V_1+V_0).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since $z=h\odot a$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}V(h+t\varepsilon z)
  =\varepsilon A(a),\quad
  \left.\frac{d^2}{dt^2}\right|_{t=0}V(h+t\varepsilon z)
  =\varepsilon^2 B(a,a).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Combining these identities with the mixed-volume derivatives above, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  A(a)=\frac{n(V_1-V_0)}{\varepsilon},
  \quad
  B(a,a)=\frac{n(n-1)(V_2-2V_1+V_0)}{\varepsilon^2}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since $S=nV_0$, the preceding identities imply
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  (n-1)\frac{A(a)^2}{S}-B(a,a)
  =\frac{n(n-1)}{V_0\varepsilon^2}
    (V_1^2-V_0V_2).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Thus $V_1^2=V_0V_2$. By \autoref{lem:sec-5-common-normal-Minkowski-equality}, $Q=\gamma P+v$, for some $\gamma>0$ and $v\in\mathbb{R}^n$. Comparison of the prescribed support values yields
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  h_i+\varepsilon h_i a_i
  =\gamma h_i+\langle v,u_i\rangle
  \quad(i\in J).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Solving these equations for $a_i$, we find
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  a_i=\frac{\gamma-1}{\varepsilon}+\frac{\langle \frac{v}{\varepsilon},u_i\rangle}{h_i}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Conversely, suppose that \eqref{eq:sec-8-irredundant-AF-equality} holds. Then $h\odot(a-c_0\mathbf{1})=\mathfrak{t}(v)$, and translation invariance of volume implies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  A(a-c_0\mathbf{1})&=0,\\
  B(a-c_0\mathbf{1},b)&=0
  \quad(b\in\mathbb{R}^J).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Together with the Euler identities, these relations imply
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  A(a)=c_0S,
  \quad
  B(a,a)=c_0^2(n-1)S.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Hence equality holds.

We now determine the kernel of $B$. If $h\odot a=\mathfrak{t}(v)$, translation invariance of volume implies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  B(a,b)=D^2V(h)[\mathfrak{t}(v),h\odot b]=0
  \quad(b\in\mathbb{R}^J).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Every such facet function belongs to the kernel:
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \{a\in\mathbb{R}^J:h\odot a\in\mathfrak{t}(\mathbb{R}^n)\}
  \subseteq\ker B.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
For the reverse inclusion, suppose that $a\in\ker B$, meaning that $B(a,b)=0$ for every $b\in\mathbb{R}^J$. Taking $b=\mathbf{1}$ and using the Euler identity $B(a,\mathbf{1})=(n-1)A(a)$ shows that $A(a)=0$, while taking $b=a$ shows that $B(a,a)=0$. Hence equality holds in \eqref{eq:sec-8-support-Hessian-inequality}, and \eqref{eq:sec-8-irredundant-AF-equality} holds for some $c_0\in\mathbb{R}$ and $v\in\mathbb{R}^n$. Here $h\odot(a-c_0\mathbf{1})=\mathfrak{t}(v)$, and translation invariance implies $A(a-c_0\mathbf{1})=0$. Since $A(\mathbf{1})=S>0$, we have $c_0=0$. Hence $h\odot a=\mathfrak{t}(v)$.

For the centered version $a^{\circ}$ defined in \eqref{eq:sec-4-centered-representative}, the Euler identities imply
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  B(a^{\circ},a^{\circ})
  =B(a,a)-(n-1)\frac{A(a)^2}{S}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Thus \eqref{eq:sec-8-support-Hessian-inequality} is equivalent to $B(a^{\circ},a^{\circ})\leq 0$. Since $A(\mathbf{1})=S>0$, we have
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathbb{R}^J=\mathbb{R}\mathbf{1}\oplus\ker A.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
This decomposition is $B$-orthogonal because
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  B(\mathbf{1},b)=(n-1)A(b)=0
  \quad(b\in\ker A).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Therefore, $B$ has exactly one positive eigenvalue.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor:sec-8-irredundant-strict-even-eigenvalue-bound}
For the antipodally indexed normals, let $h\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}^I$ satisfy $h_{-i}=h_i$ for every $i\in I^+$. Then the full-dimensional origin-symmetric polytope $P(h)$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \lambda_{1,e}(P(h))>n-1.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
First note that equality in \eqref{eq:sec-8-support-Hessian-inequality} cannot hold for a nonzero centered even facet function. Now let $a$ be a nonzero centered even facet function. Then \eqref{eq:sec-8-support-Hessian-inequality} implies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathsf{L}(a,a)=(n-1)A(a^{\odot2})-B(a,a)
  >(n-1)A(a^{\odot2}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The normalized centered even set
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \{a:a\text{ is even},\ A(a)=0,\ A(a^{\odot2})=1\}
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
is compact. Therefore, $\lambda_{1,e}(P(h))$ is strictly larger than $n-1$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-8-smooth-Dirichlet-identity}
Let $K$ and $L$ be $C^2_+$ convex bodies and $h_K>0$. Then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-smooth-Dirichlet-identity}
  (n-1)\int_{S^{n-1}}\frac{h_L^2}{h_K}\,dS_K
  -n(n-1)\mathcal{V}(K[n-2],L[2])
  =\int_{S^{n-1}}\left|\nabla\frac{h_L}{h_K}\right|_{g_K}^2\,dV_K.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
The radius-of-curvature tensor of $K+tL$ is $r_K+t r_L$. By \eqref{eq:sec-8-smooth-surface-area-measure},
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0^+}dS_{K+tL}
  =\operatorname{tr}\bigl(r_K^{-1}r_L\bigr)\,dS_K.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Differentiating \eqref{eq:sec-8-first-mixed-volume} with $M=K+tL$ at $t=0^+$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  n(n-1)\mathcal{V}(K[n-2],L[2])=\int_{S^{n-1}}h_L\operatorname{tr}\bigl(r_K^{-1}r_L\bigr)\,dS_K=\int_{S^{n-1}}\frac{h_L}{h_K}\operatorname{tr}\bigl(r_K^{-1}r_L\bigr)\,dV_K.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
By the integration-by-parts formula following \cite[Eq. (2.6)]{Mil25},
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \int_{S^{n-1}}\frac{h_L}{h_K}\operatorname{tr}\bigl(r_K^{-1}r_L\bigr)\,dV_K
  =(n-1)\int_{S^{n-1}}\frac{h_L^2}{h_K^2}\,dV_K
  -\int_{S^{n-1}}\left|\nabla\frac{h_L}{h_K}\right|_{g_K}^2\,dV_K.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-8-fixed-normal-comparison}
Let $h\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$ and $P=P(h)$. For a convex body $L$, define $z,a\in\mathbb{R}^J$ by
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  z_i=h_L(u_i),\quad a_i=\frac{z_i}{h_i}
  \quad(i\in J).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-fixed-normal-energy-comparison}
  \mathsf{L}(a,a)\leq(n-1)\int_{S^{n-1}}\frac{h_L^2}{h_P}\,dS_P
  -n(n-1)\mathcal{V}(P[n-2],L[2]).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
For $t\geq 0$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  h_{P+tL}(u_i)=h_i+t z_i
  \quad(i\in J).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
By \autoref{lem:sec-8-irredundant-open}, $h+tz\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$ whenever $\left|t\right|$ is sufficiently small. Therefore,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-fixed-normal-inclusion}
  P+tL\subseteq P(h+tz).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The $C^2$ regularity of $V$ established in \autoref{thm:sec-9-C2-regularity} ensures that $t\mapsto\operatorname{Vol}(P(h+tz))$ is $C^2$ near zero. Note that the first derivative formula in \autoref{prop:sec-8-support-derivatives} also follows from the Wulff-shape variational formula \cite[p. 412, Lem. 7.5.3]{Sch14}. Together with \eqref{eq:sec-8-first-mixed-volume} and the mixed-volume expansion \eqref{eq:sec-5-mixed-volume-expansion}, this yields
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  DV(h)[z]=\sum_{i\in J}h_L(u_i)\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(F_i)
  =n\mathcal{V}(P[n-1],L)
  =\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0^+}\operatorname{Vol}(P+tL).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since $z=h\odot a$, Taylor's formula yields
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \operatorname{Vol}(P(h+tz))
  =\operatorname{Vol}(P)+tDV(h)[z]+\frac{t^2}{2}B(a,a)+o(t^2).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
