Moreover, \eqref{eq:sec-5-mixed-volume-expansion} implies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \operatorname{Vol}(P+tL)
  &=\operatorname{Vol}(P)+nt\mathcal{V}(P[n-1],L)\\
  &\quad+\frac{n(n-1)t^2}{2}\mathcal{V}(P[n-2],L[2])+O(t^3).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
In view of \eqref{eq:sec-8-fixed-normal-inclusion},
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  0&\leq\operatorname{Vol}(P(h+tz))-\operatorname{Vol}(P+tL)\\
  &=\frac{t^2}{2}\bigl(B(a,a)-n(n-1)\mathcal{V}(P[n-2],L[2])\bigr)+o(t^2)
  \quad(t\to 0^+).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-fixed-normal-Hessian-comparison}
  B(a,a)\geq n(n-1)\mathcal{V}(P[n-2],L[2]).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{eq:sec-8-irredundant-support-forms}, \eqref{eq:sec-8-irredundant-network-formulas}, and \eqref{eq:sec-8-polytope-surface-area-measure}, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathsf{M}(a,a)=A(a^{\odot2})
  =\int_{S^{n-1}}\frac{h_L^2}{h_P}\,dS_P.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Now using $\mathsf{L}=(n-1)\mathsf{M}-B$, we conclude that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathsf{L}(a,a)\leq(n-1)\int_{S^{n-1}}\frac{h_L^2}{h_P}\,dS_P
  -n(n-1)\mathcal{V}(P[n-2],L[2]).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{rem:sec-8-Milman-upper-semicontinuity}
We compare $\lambda_{1,e}(P)$ with the mixed-volume spectral quantity $\lambda^C_{1,e}(P)$ introduced by Milman \cite[Sec. 1.4, Eq. (1.7)]{Mil24}. Milman's definition uses Putterman's mixed-volume formulation of the local $L^p$-Brunn--Minkowski inequality \cite[App. A]{Put21}.

Let $P=P(h)$ be a full-dimensional origin-symmetric polytope. For a full-dimensional origin-symmetric convex body $L$, set $a_i=h_L(u_i)/h_i$. By \eqref{eq:sec-8-polytope-surface-area-measure} and \eqref{eq:sec-8-first-mixed-volume},
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \widetilde{\mathsf{M}}(a,a)
  &=\mathsf{M}(a,a)-\frac{A(a)^2}{S}\\
  &=\int_{S^{n-1}}\frac{h_L^2}{h_P}\,dS_P
  -\frac{n\mathcal{V}(P[n-1],L)^2}{\operatorname{Vol}(P)}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The admissibility condition in Milman's definition is precisely that $a$ is nonconstant. For every admissible $L$, \eqref{eq:sec-8-fixed-normal-energy-comparison} implies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \lambda_{1,e}(P)
  \leq\frac{\mathsf{L}(a,a)}{\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}(a,a)}\leq\frac{(n-1)\displaystyle\int_{S^{n-1}}\frac{h_L^2}{h_P}\,dS_P
  -n(n-1)\mathcal{V}(P[n-2],L[2])}
  {\int_{S^{n-1}}\frac{h_L^2}{h_P}\,dS_P
  -\frac{n\mathcal{V}(P[n-1],L)^2}{\operatorname{Vol}(P)}}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Taking the infimum of the last quotient over admissible $L$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \lambda_{1,e}(P)\leq\lambda^C_{1,e}(P).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:sec-8-upper-semicontinuity}
Suppose that the full-dimensional origin-symmetric polytopes $P_{\nu}$ converge in the Hausdorff metric to an origin-symmetric $C^2_+$ convex body $K$. Then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-upper-semicontinuity}
  \limsup_{\nu\to\infty}\lambda_{1,e}(P_{\nu})\leq\lambda_{1,e}(K).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Let $\psi\in C^2(S^{n-1})$ be a nonzero even function satisfying
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \int_{S^{n-1}}\psi\,dV_K=0.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Choose $c_0>0$ sufficiently large that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  h_K(c_0+\psi)> 0,\quad
  r_{h_K(c_0+\psi)}=c_0 r_K+r_{h_K\psi}
  \quad\text{is positive definite}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Therefore, $h_K(c_0+\psi)$ is the support function of a $C^2_+$ origin-symmetric convex body $L$.

For each $\nu$, we index the facets of $P_{\nu}$ by a signed set $I_{\nu}$, with outer unit normals satisfying $u_{-i}^{(\nu)}=-u_i^{(\nu)}$. Define
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  a_i^{(\nu)}=\frac{h_L(u_i^{(\nu)})}{h_{P_{\nu}}(u_i^{(\nu)})}
  \quad(i\in I_{\nu}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since $h_L$ and $h_{P_{\nu}}$ are even, $a^{(\nu)}$ is an even facet function. Let $\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_{\nu}$ and $\mathsf{L}_{\nu}$ be the centered mass and energy forms of $P_{\nu}$. By \eqref{eq:sec-8-irredundant-support-forms}, \eqref{eq:sec-8-irredundant-network-formulas}, \eqref{eq:sec-8-first-mixed-volume}, and \eqref{eq:sec-8-fixed-normal-energy-comparison}, the centered mass and energy satisfy
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-approximating-mass-energy}
  \widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_{\nu}(a^{(\nu)},a^{(\nu)})&=\int_{S^{n-1}}\frac{h_L^2}{h_{P_{\nu}}}\,dS_{P_{\nu}}
  -\frac{n\mathcal{V}(P_{\nu}[n-1],L)^2}{\operatorname{Vol}(P_{\nu})},\\
  \mathsf{L}_{\nu}(a^{(\nu)},a^{(\nu)})&\leq(n-1)\int_{S^{n-1}}\frac{h_L^2}{h_{P_{\nu}}}\,dS_{P_{\nu}}
  -n(n-1)\mathcal{V}(P_{\nu}[n-2],L[2]).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Under Hausdorff convergence, $h_{P_{\nu}}\to h_K>0$ uniformly and $S_{P_{\nu}}\rightharpoonup S_K$. Consequently,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \frac{h_L^2}{h_{P_{\nu}}}&\to\frac{h_L^2}{h_K}
  \quad\text{uniformly on }S^{n-1},\\
  \int_{S^{n-1}}\frac{h_L^2}{h_{P_{\nu}}}\,dS_{P_{\nu}}
  &\to\int_{S^{n-1}}\frac{h_L^2}{h_K}\,dS_K.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since mixed volumes are continuous under Hausdorff convergence, passing to the limit in the first relation of \eqref{eq:sec-8-approximating-mass-energy} and using $dV_K=h_K\,dS_K$ and $h_L/h_K=c_0+\psi$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-mass-limit}
  \lim_{\nu\to\infty}\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_{\nu}(a^{(\nu)},a^{(\nu)})=\int_{S^{n-1}}(c_0+\psi)^2\,dV_K
  -\frac{\left(\int_{S^{n-1}}(c_0+\psi)\,dV_K\right)^2}
        {\int_{S^{n-1}}1\,dV_K}=\int_{S^{n-1}}\psi^2\,dV_K.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Similarly, passing to the $\limsup$ in the second relation of \eqref{eq:sec-8-approximating-mass-energy} and using \autoref{lem:sec-8-smooth-Dirichlet-identity},
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-8-energy-limit}
  \limsup_{\nu\to\infty}\mathsf{L}_{\nu}(a^{(\nu)},a^{(\nu)})\leq\int_{S^{n-1}}\left|\nabla\psi\right|_{g_K}^2\,dV_K.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:sec-8-mass-limit}, the centered mass of $a^{(\nu)}$ is positive for all sufficiently large $\nu$. The Rayleigh characterization \eqref{eq:introduction-Rayleigh-characterization}, together with \eqref{eq:sec-8-mass-limit} and \eqref{eq:sec-8-energy-limit}, implies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \limsup_{\nu\to\infty}\lambda_{1,e}(P_{\nu})
  \leq\limsup_{\nu\to\infty}
  \frac{\mathsf{L}_{\nu}(a^{(\nu)},a^{(\nu)})}
       {\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_{\nu}(a^{(\nu)},a^{(\nu)})}
       \leq\frac{\displaystyle\int_{S^{n-1}}\left|\nabla\psi\right|_{g_K}^2\,dV_K}
       {\displaystyle\int_{S^{n-1}}\psi^2\,dV_K}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Taking the infimum over $\psi$ in the smooth Rayleigh characterization \eqref{eq:introduction-smooth-Rayleigh-characterization} proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-9-linear-mixed-volume}
Suppose that $h,r\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$ belong to the closure of a common simple type chamber. For $0\leq k\leq n-1$, the function
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  q\mapsto\mathcal{V}(P(q),P(h)[n-1-k],P(r)[k])
  \quad(q\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}})
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
is the restriction of a linear functional on $\mathbb{R}^J$.
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
By \autoref{lem:sec-3-type-chamber-Minkowski-addition},
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  P(h)+sP(r)=P(h+sr)\quad(s\geq 0).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Every facet normal of this polytope is among the prescribed normals. From the first mixed-volume formula \eqref{eq:sec-8-first-mixed-volume} and Minkowski multilinearity, it follows that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  &\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{n-1}{k}s^k
    \mathcal{V}(P(q),P(h)[n-1-k],P(r)[k])\\
  &\quad=\mathcal{V}(P(q),(P(h)+sP(r))[n-1])\\
  &\quad=\frac1n\sum_{i\in J}q_i\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(P(h+sr)^{u_i})
  \quad(s\geq 0).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
For every fixed $s$, the last expression is linear in $q$. The coefficients of a polynomial of degree at most $n-1$ are linear combinations of its values at $n$ distinct nonnegative numbers. Hence every coefficient on the first line is linear in $q$.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:sec-9-C2-regularity}
The volume function belongs to $C^2(\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}})$.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
For $h\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$, we choose $\mathcal{U}$ as in \eqref{eq:sec-9-local-incident-chambers} and define
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{Q}_h(q,r)=n(n-1)\mathcal{V}(P(q),P(r),P(h)[n-2])
  \quad(q,r\in \mathcal{U}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
For each fixed $r\in\mathcal{U}$, the vectors $h$ and $r$ lie in the closure of a common simple type chamber. Thus \autoref{lem:sec-9-linear-mixed-volume} shows that $q\mapsto\mathcal{Q}_h(q,r)$ is the restriction of a linear functional on $\mathbb{R}^J$. By symmetry, $r\mapsto\mathcal{Q}_h(q,r)$ has the same property for each fixed $q\in\mathcal{U}$. Since $\mathcal{U}$ contains a basis of $\mathbb{R}^J$, the map $\mathcal{Q}_h:\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{U}\to\mathbb{R}$ extends uniquely to a symmetric bilinear form on $\mathbb{R}^J\times\mathbb{R}^J$, still denoted by $\mathcal{Q}_h$.

We first compare the derivatives of the incident chamber volume polynomials at $h$. If $\sigma$ is incident to $h$ and $q\in\sigma\cap \mathcal{U}$, then $h+sq\in\sigma$ for $s>0$. By \autoref{lem:sec-3-type-chamber-Minkowski-addition},
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \widehat{V}_{\sigma}(h+sq)=\operatorname{Vol}(P(h)+sP(q))
  =\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk s^k\mathcal{V}(P(q)[k],P(h)[n-k]).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Comparing the constant, linear, and quadratic coefficients, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \widehat{V}_{\sigma}(h)&=V(h),\\
  D\widehat{V}_{\sigma}(h)[q]&=\sum_{i\in J}q_i\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(P(h)^{u_i})
    =:\mathcal{L}_h[q],\\
  D^2\widehat{V}_{\sigma}(h)[q,q]&=\mathcal{Q}_h(q,q).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The set $\sigma\cap\mathcal{U}$ is nonempty and open. The polynomial identity principle and polarization show that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-9-common-chamber-derivatives}
  \widehat{V}_{\sigma}(h)=V(h),\quad
  D\widehat{V}_{\sigma}(h)=\mathcal{L}_h,\quad
  D^2\widehat{V}_{\sigma}(h)=\mathcal{Q}_h
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
for every chamber incident to $h$.

We now verify differentiability of $V$. For sufficiently small $z$, some chamber $\sigma_z$ is incident to both $h$ and $h+z$. Applying Taylor's formula and using \eqref{eq:sec-9-common-chamber-derivatives}, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  V(h+z)=\widehat{V}_{\sigma_z}(h+z)
  =V(h)+\mathcal{L}_h[z]+\frac12\mathcal{Q}_h(z,z)+O(\lvert z\rvert^3).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The remainder is uniform because only finitely many chamber polynomials occur. Thus $DV(h)=\mathcal{L}_h$. Since $h$ was arbitrary, $V$ is differentiable on $\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$, and its derivative agrees with the derivative of every incident branch.

Similarly, Taylor's formula for the first derivatives reads
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  DV(h+z)=D\widehat{V}_{\sigma_z}(h+z)
  =\mathcal{L}_h+\mathcal{Q}_h(z,\mathord{\cdot})+O(\lvert z\rvert^2).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The remainder is measured in the norm on $(\mathbb{R}^J)^*$, with a constant independent of $\sigma_z$. Since $DV(h)=\mathcal{L}_h$, there is a constant $C>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \frac{\left\|DV(h+z)-DV(h)-\mathcal{Q}_h(z,\mathord{\cdot})\right\|}{\lvert z\rvert}\leq C\lvert z\rvert\to 0\quad(z\to 0,\ z\neq 0).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Hence $z\mapsto\mathcal{Q}_h(z,\mathord{\cdot})$ is the Fr\'echet derivative of $DV$ at $h$. In particular, $D^2V(h)=\mathcal{Q}_h$. Since $h$ was arbitrary, $V$ is twice differentiable on $\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$. By \eqref{eq:sec-9-common-chamber-derivatives},
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-9-incident-Hessian-agreement}
  D^2V(q)=\mathcal{Q}_q=D^2\widehat{V}_{\sigma}(q)\quad(q\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}\cap\overline{\sigma}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Finally, for $h+z\in\mathcal{U}$, we choose a simple type chamber $\sigma_z$ incident to both $h$ and $h+z$, as in \eqref{eq:sec-9-local-incident-chambers}. By \eqref{eq:sec-9-incident-Hessian-agreement},
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left\|D^2V(h+z)-D^2V(h)\right\|
  &=\left\|D^2\widehat{V}_{\sigma_z}(h+z)-D^2\widehat{V}_{\sigma_z}(h)\right\|\\
  &\leq\max_{\sigma:\,h\in\overline{\sigma}}
    \left\|D^2\widehat{V}_{\sigma}(h+z)-D^2\widehat{V}_{\sigma}(h)\right\|\to 0\quad(z\to 0).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Therefore, $V\in C^2(\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}})$.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:sec-9-support-Hessian-midpoint}
For every $h\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}$ and $w,z\in\mathbb{R}^J$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-9-support-Hessian-midpoint}
  \liminf_{t\to 0^+}
  \frac{D^2V(h+tz)[w,w]+D^2V(h-tz)[w,w]-2D^2V(h)[w,w]}{t}\geq 0.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
For $t>0$ sufficiently small, write
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \Delta_t(u,v)
  =D^2V(h+tz)[u,v]+D^2V(h-tz)[u,v]-2D^2V(h)[u,v].
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
We first use mixed volumes to bound $\Delta_t(h+\varepsilon w,h+\varepsilon w)$ from below for a fixed sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$. We then apply bilinearity to obtain the required bound for $\Delta_t(w,w)$.

Let $\mathcal{U}$ be as in \eqref{eq:sec-9-local-incident-chambers}, and fix $\varepsilon>0$ such that $\tilde{h}=h+\varepsilon w\in \mathcal{U}$. Thus $h$ and $\tilde{h}$ belong to the closure of a common simple type chamber. We write $P=P(h)$, $L=P(\tilde{h})$, and
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \Psi(q)=n(n-1)\mathcal{V}(P(q)[n-2],L[2])
  \quad(q\in \mathcal{U}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since $h_L(u_i)=\tilde{h}_i$, \eqref{eq:sec-8-fixed-normal-Hessian-comparison} implies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-9-Hessian-lower-comparison}
  D^2V(q)[\tilde{h},\tilde{h}]\geq\Psi(q).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
At $q=h$, equality follows by differentiating $V(h+s\tilde{h})=\operatorname{Vol}(P+sL)$ twice at $s=0$, using \autoref{lem:sec-3-type-chamber-Minkowski-addition}:
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-9-Hessian-comparison-contact}
  D^2V(h)[\tilde{h},\tilde{h}]=\Psi(h).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

We next show that the first-order terms of $\Psi$ along the two rays $h\pm tz$ cancel. When $n=2$, this holds because $\Psi$ is constant. For $n\geq 3$, by \autoref{lem:sec-9-linear-mixed-volume} with $r=\tilde{h}$ and $k=2$, there is a linear functional $\ell$ on $\mathbb{R}^J$ such that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \ell(q)=n(n-1)(n-2)\mathcal{V}(P(q),P[n-3],L[2])
  \quad(q\in \mathcal{U}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Fix $\tau>0$ small enough that $q_{\pm}=h\pm\tau z\in \mathcal{U}$. Each of $q_+$ and $q_-$ shares a simple chamber closure with $h$. Hence \autoref{lem:sec-3-type-chamber-Minkowski-addition} implies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  P(h\pm tz)=\left(1-\frac{t}{\tau}\right)P+\frac{t}{\tau}P(q_{\pm})
  \quad(0\leq t\leq\tau).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
By multilinearity,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \Psi(h\pm tz)
  &=\Psi(h)+\frac{t}{\tau}\bigl(\ell(q_{\pm})-\ell(h)\bigr)+O(t^2)\\
  &=\Psi(h)\pm t\ell(z)+O(t^2).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Thus, in every dimension $n\geq 2$, the sum $\Psi(h+tz)+\Psi(h-tz)-2\Psi(h)$ is $O(t^2)$. The lower bound \eqref{eq:sec-9-Hessian-lower-comparison} and equality \eqref{eq:sec-9-Hessian-comparison-contact} imply, for some $C\geq 0$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-9-comparison-midpoint}
  \Delta_t(\tilde{h},\tilde{h})
  \geq\Psi(h+tz)+\Psi(h-tz)-2\Psi(h)\geq-Ct^2.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

For every fixed $v\in\mathbb{R}^J$, Euler's identity and the $C^2$ regularity from \autoref{thm:sec-9-C2-regularity} imply
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  D^2V(h+sz)[h,v]
  &=(n-1)DV(h+sz)[v]-sD^2V(h+sz)[z,v]\\
  &=D^2V(h)[h,v]+(n-2)sD^2V(h)[z,v]+o(\lvert s\rvert).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Adding the expansions for $s=t$ and $s=-t$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-9-radial-Hessian-midpoint}
  \Delta_t(h,v)=o(t)
  \quad(t\to 0^+).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since $\tilde{h}=h+\varepsilon w$, we expand by bilinearity:
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \varepsilon^2\Delta_t(w,w)
  =\Delta_t(\tilde{h},\tilde{h})-\Delta_t(h,h)-2\varepsilon\Delta_t(h,w)
  \geq-Ct^2+o(t).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Dividing by $\varepsilon^2t$ and taking the lower limit proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor:sec-10-symmetric-chamber-selection}
Let $h\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}^I$ be even. For every even facet function $f$, there is a symmetric simple type chamber $\sigma$ incident to $h$ such that $ h+t(h\odot f)\in\overline{\sigma}$ for all sufficiently small $t\geq 0$.
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{U}$ be as in \eqref{eq:sec-9-local-incident-chambers} and choose $\tau>0$ such that $q=h+\tau(h\odot f)\in\mathcal{U}$. By \eqref{eq:sec-8-symmetric-local-chamber-covering}, there is a symmetric simple type chamber $\sigma$ with $h,q\in\overline{\sigma}$. Convexity of $\overline{\sigma}$ implies $h+t(h\odot f)\in\overline{\sigma}$ for $0\leq t\leq\tau$.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
\label{cor:sec-10-signed-support-Hessian-regularity}
The volume function satisfies
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  V\in C^2(\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}^I).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
If $h\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}^I$ and $\sigma$ is a simple type chamber incident to $h$, then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-10-signed-incident-derivative-agreement}
  D^r\widehat{V}_{\sigma}(h)=D^rV(h)
  \quad(0\leq r\leq 2).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Moreover, for every $h\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{irr}}^I$ and $w,z\in\mathbb{R}^I$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-10-signed-support-Hessian-midpoint}
  \liminf_{t\to 0^+}
  \frac{
    D^2V(h+tz)[w,w]+D^2V(h-tz)[w,w]-2D^2V(h)[w,w]
  }{t}
  \geq 0.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
This is the antipodal version of \autoref{thm:sec-9-C2-regularity}, \eqref{eq:sec-9-common-chamber-derivatives}, \eqref{eq:sec-9-incident-Hessian-agreement}, and \autoref{thm:sec-9-support-Hessian-midpoint}.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:sec-10-incident-chamber-Rayleigh-stationarity}
For every symmetric simple type chamber $\sigma$ incident to $h$ and every $g\in\mathcal{E}^+$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-10-chamber-stationarity}
  C_{\sigma}(f,f,g)
  =\beta\bigl\{B(f^{\odot2},g)
    -A(f^{\odot2}\odot g)\bigr\}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
For every $g\in\mathcal{E}^+$, define $h_t=h\odot(\mathbf{1}+tg)$. Then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{0^+}
  \frac{\mathsf{L}_{h_t}(f,f)}
  {\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_{h_t}(f,f)}=0.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
For $q\in\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{E}^+$, define
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  f_q=f-\frac{A_q(f)}{S_q}\mathbf{1}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Then $A_q(f_q)=0$, and the centered Rayleigh quotient of $f_q$ at $q$ is
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{R}(q)
  =\frac{\mathsf{L}_q(f,f)}{\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_q(f,f)}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The Rayleigh principle and local minimality imply
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-10-Rayleigh-sandwich}
  \mathcal{R}(q)
  &\geq \lambda_{1,e}(P(q))
  \geq \lambda
  =\mathcal{R}(h).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Let $\sigma$ be a symmetric simple type chamber incident to $h$. In view of \eqref{eq:sec-8-even-incident-neighborhood}, the set $\mathcal{U}\cap\sigma\cap\mathcal{E}^+$ is nonempty and relatively open in $\mathcal{E}^+$. Consider the set of points $q$ in this intersection satisfying
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-10-reflected-support-slacks}
\delta_{\tilde{J},k}(2h-q)\neq 0
\quad(\tilde{J}\in\mathcal{B},\ k\notin\tilde{J}).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since each slack restricts to a nonzero linear functional on $\mathcal{E}^+$, this set is nonempty and relatively open. For $q$ in this set, define
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  z=q-h=h\odot a,
  \quad
  a_i=\frac{q_i-h_i}{h_i}\quad(i\in I).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Since $h\in\overline{\sigma}$ and $\sigma$ is open and convex, $h+tz=(1-t)h+tq\in\sigma\cap\mathcal{U}$ for $0<t\leq 1$.

Also note that the reflected support vector $q_-=2h-q$ belongs to $\mathcal{U}$ and, by \eqref{eq:sec-10-reflected-support-slacks}, to a support cell $\mathfrak{C}$. Let $\tilde{\sigma}=\sigma_{\mathfrak{C}}$ denote the chamber that contains $q_-$. By \eqref{eq:sec-9-local-incident-chambers}, $h\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}\cap\mathcal{U}$, and hence $h\in\overline{\tilde{\sigma}}$. Convexity implies $h-tz=(1-t)h+tq_-\in\tilde{\sigma}\cap\mathcal{U}$ for $0<t\leq 1$.

As in \eqref{eq:sec-6-eigen-equations}, the eigenvalue equation extends to every $k\in\mathcal{E}^+$:
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  B(f,k)=\beta A(f\odot k)
  \quad(k\in\mathcal{E}^+).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Using \eqref{eq:sec-10-signed-incident-derivative-agreement}, we apply the calculation in the proof of \autoref{lem:sec-6-rayleigh-stationarity} to $\widehat{V}_{\sigma}$ along $h+tz$ and to $\widehat{V}_{\tilde{\sigma}}$ along $h-tz$:
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{0^+}\mathcal{R}(h+tz)
  &=\beta\bigl\{B(f^{\odot2},a)-A(f^{\odot2}\odot a)\bigr\}
    -C_{\sigma}(f,f,a),\\
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{0^+}\mathcal{R}(h-tz)
  &=-\beta\bigl\{B(f^{\odot2},a)-A(f^{\odot2}\odot a)\bigr\}
    +C_{\tilde{\sigma}}(f,f,a).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

For $\xi=h\odot f$, Taylor expansion of the two chamber polynomials at $h$ leads to
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  D^2V(h+tz)[\xi,\xi]
  &=D^2V(h)[\xi,\xi]+tC_{\sigma}(f,f,a)+O(t^2),\\
  D^2V(h-tz)[\xi,\xi]
  &=D^2V(h)[\xi,\xi]-tC_{\tilde{\sigma}}(f,f,a)+O(t^2).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The midpoint inequality \eqref{eq:sec-10-signed-support-Hessian-midpoint} now implies $C_{\sigma}(f,f,a)\geq C_{\tilde{\sigma}}(f,f,a)$. Hence
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{0^+}\mathcal{R}(h+tz)
  +\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{0^+}\mathcal{R}(h-tz)
  &=C_{\tilde{\sigma}}(f,f,a)-C_{\sigma}(f,f,a)
  \leq 0.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Both derivatives are nonnegative. Consequently, both vanish.

Thus, for every $q\in\mathcal{U}\cap\sigma\cap\mathcal{E}^+$ satisfying \eqref{eq:sec-10-reflected-support-slacks} and $a_i=(q_i-h_i)/h_i$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \beta\bigl\{B(f^{\odot2},a)-A(f^{\odot2}\odot a)\bigr\}
  -C_{\sigma}(f,f,a)=0.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
The map $q\mapsto((q_i-h_i)/h_i)_{i\in I}$ is an affine isomorphism of $\mathcal{E}^+$ which maps the restricted set of support vectors onto a nonempty relatively open subset of $\mathcal{E}^+$. Since the left-hand side is linear in $a$ and vanishes on this image,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  C_{\sigma}(f,f,g)=\beta\bigl\{B(f^{\odot2},g)-A(f^{\odot2}\odot g)\bigr\}\quad (g\in\mathcal{E}^+).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

Finally, let $g\in\mathcal{E}^+$. By \autoref{cor:sec-10-symmetric-chamber-selection}, there is a symmetric simple type chamber $\sigma_g$ incident to $h$ such that $h_t\in\overline{\sigma_g}\cap\mathcal{U}$ for all sufficiently small $t\geq 0$. Applying \eqref{eq:sec-10-signed-incident-derivative-agreement} at $h_t$, the calculation in the proof of \autoref{lem:sec-6-rayleigh-stationarity}, and \eqref{eq:sec-10-chamber-stationarity} for $\sigma_g$, the claim follows.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec-10-incident-fourth-derivative-lower-bound}
Assume that $n\geq 3$. There is a unique $\eta\in\mathcal{E}^+$ such that, for every symmetric simple type chamber $\sigma$ incident to $h$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-10-incident-eta}
  C_{\sigma}(f,f,g)=(n-2)B(\eta,g)
  \quad(g\in\mathcal{E}^+).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
For every such $\sigma$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-10-incident-fourth-bound}
  D_{\sigma}(f,f,f,f)
  \geq (n-2)(n-3)B(\eta,\eta).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
The proof of \autoref{cor:sec-8-irredundant-strict-even-eigenvalue-bound} shows that $B$ is negative definite on $\mathcal{E}_0^+$. The argument following \eqref{eq:sec-7-eta-definition} then shows that $B:\mathcal{E}^+\to(\mathcal{E}^+)^*$ is an isomorphism. By \eqref{eq:sec-10-chamber-stationarity}, the functional $C_{\sigma}(f,f,\mathord{\cdot})$ on $\mathcal{E}^+$ is independent of $\sigma$. Hence a unique $\eta\in\mathcal{E}^+$ satisfies \eqref{eq:sec-10-incident-eta} for every symmetric simple type chamber $\sigma$ incident to $h$.

When $n=3$, both sides of \eqref{eq:sec-10-incident-fourth-bound} are zero. Assume now that $n\geq 4$.

Fix such a chamber $\sigma$ and choose $q\in\mathcal{U}\cap\sigma\cap\mathcal{E}^+$, which is possible by \eqref{eq:sec-8-even-incident-neighborhood}. Since $h\in\overline{\sigma}$ and $\sigma$ is convex,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  q_s=(1-s)h+sq
  \in\sigma\cap\mathcal{E}^+\cap(0,\infty)^I
  \quad(0<s\leq 1)
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
and $q_s\to h$ as $s\to 0^+$.

Let $B_{\sigma}^s$, $C_{\sigma}^s$, and $D_{\sigma}^s$ denote the logarithmic support forms of $\widehat{V}_{\sigma}$ at $q_s$. Continuity of the derivatives of $\widehat{V}_{\sigma}$ and \eqref{eq:sec-10-signed-incident-derivative-agreement} show that, as $s\to 0^+$,
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  B_{\sigma}^s&\to B,\\
  C_{\sigma}^s(f,f,\mathord{\cdot})&\to C_{\sigma}(f,f,\mathord{\cdot}),\\
  D_{\sigma}^s(f,f,f,f)&\to D_{\sigma}(f,f,f,f).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
For all sufficiently small $s>0$, the map $B_{\sigma}^s:\mathcal{E}^+\to(\mathcal{E}^+)^*$ is also an isomorphism. Let $\eta^s\in\mathcal{E}^+$ be the unique vector satisfying
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-10-incident-eta-approximation}
  C_{\sigma}^s(f,f,g)=(n-2)B_{\sigma}^s(\eta^s,g)
  \quad(g\in\mathcal{E}^+).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Viewing $B$ and $B_{\sigma}^s$ as maps from $\mathcal{E}^+$ to $(\mathcal{E}^+)^*$, we have
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \eta
  =\frac{1}{n-2}B^{-1}\bigl(C_{\sigma}(f,f,\mathord{\cdot})\bigr),
  \quad
  \eta^s
  =\frac{1}{n-2}(B_{\sigma}^s)^{-1}
  \bigl(C_{\sigma}^s(f,f,\mathord{\cdot})\bigr).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Therefore $\eta^s\to\eta$.

By \autoref{lem:sec-3-antipodal-volume-parity}, both sides of \eqref{eq:sec-10-incident-eta-approximation} vanish for odd $g$. Thus this equation holds for every facet function $g$. The construction in \eqref{eq:sec-7-Gamma-definition} and the argument following it apply to $\widehat{V}_{\sigma}$ at $q_s$, and the corresponding class is primitive. The argument of \autoref{lem:sec-7-fourth-derivative-lower-bound} then establishes
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  D_{\sigma}^s(f,f,f,f)
  \geq (n-2)(n-3)B_{\sigma}^s(\eta^s,\eta^s).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Letting $s\to 0^+$ proves the second claim.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- corollary-7: body_md -->
\label{cor:sec-10-incident-trial-Rayleigh-second-variation}
Assume that $n\geq 3$. For $t\geq 0$ sufficiently small, set
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  h_t=h\odot(\mathbf{1}+tf),\quad
  u_t=f-(1+\beta)t f^{\odot2},
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
and define
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}(t)
  =\frac{\mathsf{L}_{h_t}(u_t,u_t)}
  {\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_{h_t}(u_t,u_t)}.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Then $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}(0)=\lambda$, and its right derivatives satisfy
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-10-incident-branch-second-variation}
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}'(0^+)=0,\quad
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0^+)
  \leq \frac{2\beta\lambda}{S(n-1)}
  \bigl(1+(n-1)\beta\bigr).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Moreover, if $-1/(n-1)<\beta<0$, then
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}(t)<\lambda
  \quad\text{for all sufficiently small }t>0.
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-7 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
In view of \autoref{cor:sec-10-symmetric-chamber-selection}, we may choose a symmetric simple type chamber $\sigma$ incident to $h$ such that $h_t\in\overline{\sigma}\cap\mathcal{U}$ for all sufficiently small $t\geq 0$. By \eqref{eq:sec-10-signed-incident-derivative-agreement} at $h_t$, the quotient $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}(t)$ agrees on this interval with the quotient determined by $\widehat{V}_{\sigma}$. Since $\widehat{V}_{\sigma}$ is a polynomial and $\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}_h(f,f)=1$, the chamber quotient provides a real-analytic extension of $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}$ across zero.

By \eqref{eq:sec-10-chamber-stationarity}, the calculation leading to \eqref{eq:sec-6-exact-second-derivative} applies here with $d_4=D_{\sigma}(f,f,f,f)$. Hence $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}'(0^+)=0$ and
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}\label{eq:sec-10-exact-incident-trial-second-derivative}
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0^+)
  =-\beta(2\beta-1)(\beta+2)A(f^{\odot4})
  +3\beta^2B(f^{\odot2},f^{\odot2})
  -D_{\sigma}(f,f,f,f).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
Using \eqref{eq:sec-10-chamber-stationarity} and \autoref{lem:sec-10-incident-fourth-derivative-lower-bound}, the calculation in the proof of \autoref{lem:sec-7-trial-Rayleigh-second-variation-bound} yields
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0^+)
  \leq\frac{2\beta\lambda}{S(n-1)}
  \bigl(1+(n-1)\beta\bigr).
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}

If $-1/(n-1)<\beta<0$, then \eqref{eq:sec-10-incident-branch-second-variation} implies $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0^+)<0$. Since $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}(0)=\lambda$ and $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}'(0^+)=0$, Taylor's formula now shows that
\begin{equation}\begin{alignedat}{2}
  \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}(t)-\lambda
  =\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}''(0^+)t^2+o(t^2)<0
\end{alignedat}\end{equation}
for all sufficiently small $t>0$.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
By \autoref{cor:sec-8-irredundant-strict-even-eigenvalue-bound}, $\beta<0$. If the estimate failed, then $-\frac{1}{n-1}<\beta<0$. For all sufficiently small $t>0$, \autoref{cor:sec-10-incident-trial-Rayleigh-second-variation}, the Rayleigh principle, and local minimality imply $\lambda\leq\lambda_{1,e}(P(h_t))\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{tr}}(t)<\lambda$, a contradiction.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
